{"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c0ljzye1zgno", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Sydney police plead for return of man, 85, kidnapped by mistake \n![Supplied An elderly man with grey hair and wearing a dark blue jumper smiles at the camera.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/9db6/live/4a163370-0aea-11f1-b38f-dfbde52a66fe.jpg.webp)Supplied\nPolice say Chris Baghsarian, 85, was kidnapped from his home in the Sydney suburb of North Ryde by mistake.\nSydney police have appealed for the return of an 85-year-old whom they believe was kidnapped by mistake.\nChris Baghsarian, who lived alone, was taken from his house in the suburb of North Ryde by at least three attackers early on Friday morning, and bundled into a dark-coloured SUV. \n\"They were intending to take somebody, but have taken the wrong person,\" Det Acting Supt Andrew Marks told reporters on Monday, adding that no ransom note had been received.\n\"The family are in distress. All they want is their father to be returned, their grandfather to be returned,\" he said, appealing for people to report any unusual activity seen around properties known to be empty.\nThe appeal came as the Sydney Morning Herald (SMH) reported that videos and images of a severely injured Baghsarian in an unknown location were circulating in Sydney's underworld.\n\"It's not ideal that those images are out there,\" Marks said, noting that Baghsarian needed daily medication and that \"every hour is very important\".\n\"I have seen the videos,\" he add. \"They are distressing to see an 85-year-old man in that condition.\"\nHe said that in a \"normal\" kidnapping case, \"we would be aware of a ransom\". \n\"But because the family are not involved in this type of crime, and they're not involved in that world, they haven't received a ransom.\" \nCases of mistaken identity have become increasingly common in Sydney's underworld, the SMH reported, as large organised crime networks increasingly subcontract crimes.\nLast year a 23-year-old plumber was shot dead in his driveway in Condell Park in what police believe was also a case of mistaken identity.\nHe was \"a million per cent confident they have the wrong person,\" Marks said but would not elaborate on who police thought the intended target was.\n\"The fact that these offenders \u2013 these kidnappers \u2013 have got it so wrong is concerning.\"\nCCTV footage published by local media showed an SUV driving up outside Baghsarian's home on Friday. Two hooded figures then approach the house before reappearing with someone who is struggling in their arms and then loaded into the SUV.\nA burnt out vehicle matching the description of the getaway car was found in the nearby suburb of South Turramurra shortly afterwards.\nPolice said Baghsarian was wearing grey pajamas and a red and blue flannelette shirt when he was kidnapped.\nNew South Wales Premier Chris Minns has also pleaded for Baghsarian's safe return.\n\"Let police know where he's currently located or alternatively drop Mr Baghsarian off at a shopping centre or an emergency department or even a nursing home,\" he appealed to the kidnappers. \"We urge them to do that as soon as possible.\"\n[Asia](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c5rznn0nvvyt)\n[Australia](https://www.bbc.com/news/world/australia)\n* * *\nRelated\n## ['Dragged out and set on fire' - the Bangladesh mob killing that shocked the world](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cj3vve6v17xo)\n## [After the landslide: Can India reset ties with a BNP-led Bangladesh?](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cd6z007wgg6o)\n## [Is this a 'very Chinese time in your life'? {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cn87gj9k301o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Stroke survivors trial new at-home tech: 'It's given me my freedom back'\n![BBC Amanda, our case study, is seated wearing a grey tracksuit with her hand raised.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/9107/live/ed515dc0-08ed-11f1-94dc-adc7436c6c75.jpg.webp)BBC\nAmanda James-Hammett was just 37 when she had a stroke while doing the dishes at home.\n\"I heard a pop in my head, like a big bubble,\" she said. \n\"I tried to scream, but it wasn't a normal scream. I knew something wasn't right.\"\nWithin hours, Amanda had lost the ability to speak and move her right arm. She would later have to relearn how to talk, read and carry out everyday tasks.\nSix years on, she has participated in a nationwide NHS trial testing a new \"at home\" device that it's hoped could help stroke survivors regain hand and arm movement.\n\"It's about freedom,\" Amanda says. \"It's given me my freedom back.\" \nThe technology is being tested at 19 NHS sites across the UK, led by Sheffield Teaching Hospitals NHS Foundation Trust in partnership with the University of Sheffield.\nResearchers behind the \u00a32m study - known as the Triceps trial - say it is the largest trial of brain stimulation and stroke ever conducted. It aims to recruit around 270 people.\n\"Stroke is an interruption to the blood supply to the brain,\" says Dr Sheharyar Baig, a neurologist at Sheffield's Royal Hallamshire Hospital.\n\"When the blood supply is interrupted, unfortunately the brain stops functioning in a certain area. That can lead to all manner of symptoms, from weakness to visual problems and speech problems.\"\nSigns can include:\n  * Face weakness \u2013 it might be hard to smile, and one side of your face may droop\n  * Arm weakness \u2013 you may not be able to fully lift both arms and keep them there because of weakness or numbness in one arm\n  * Speech problems \u2013 you may slur your words or sound confused\n\n\nPeople displaying any of these symptoms are advised to call 999.\nDr Baig says stroke is \"the leading cause of adult-onset disability in the UK\", affecting about 100,000 people a year.\nMore than a million people living with its long term effects, he says.\nAround half of stroke survivors experience ongoing arm weakness, which can range from loss of dexterity to no movement at all.\nIt can make everyday tasks such as dressing, cooking and working difficult. \nWith a knock-on effect of adding to pressure on health and social care services, finding ways to help people shift their recovery from hospital to their own homes can be vital.\n## How the device works\nParticipants in the Triceps trial use a small electrical device that sits inside the ear and stimulates the vagus nerve - a major nerve connecting the brain and abdomen - while carrying out rehabilitation exercises.\n![A watch, smartphone and the portable device with an ear piece on a table. The smartphone displays an interface to control the device, saying \"service stopped\" and \"press 'start\".](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4429/live/359bfe10-08e3-11f1-8adf-6f6ee42cf6d7.jpg.webp)\nThe device, resembling a wired earphone, sits in the ear. It works alongside a portable device worn on the wrist, connected to smartphone.\n\"The recovery process is unfortunately quite slow for many people,\" Dr Baig says, adding it can involve lots of effort and hours of rehabilitation.\n\"So we're interested in ways we can boost the effects of rehab and create a brain environment that's more responsive to it.\"\nUnlike earlier versions of vagus nerve stimulation, which required surgery to implant a device, this treatment is non-invasive and can be used at home.\nDr Baig adds the pulses sent by the ear piece are set at a level to operate comfortably and without pain.\n## 'A new woman'\nAmanda used the device for up to an hour a day as part of her rehab over 12 weeks, combining it with exercises and everyday tasks.\n\"At first I didn't think it was going to work at all,\" she says. \n\"But after a couple of weeks, I started to see changes in my hand.\"\nOne of the biggest milestones for her was returning to sewing \u2013 a passion she could not initially pursue after her stroke.\n![Amanda is sitting at a white sewing machine, with some fabric in her hand. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/51d6/live/08a1eed0-08e1-11f1-961c-5b7d58c0375b.jpg.webp)\nAmanda is back in the sewing room \nAmanda says she feels like \"a new woman\" now that she can cut material and use her sewing machine again.\n\"I can put my socks on, I can do my shoes, I can do my house,\" she tells the BBC. \n\"I don't have to wait for anyone else.\"\n## Early improvements\nSo far, more than 200 people have taken part in the trial. \nWhile researchers do not yet know who is receiving active stimulation and who is receiving placebo, they say early signs are promising.\n\"We have seen some wonderful improvements in people's arm function,\" Dr Baig says.\nBut he also stresses the treatment is not a cure, rather something they hope will help people in everyday life.\n\"Somebody who was unable to carry a cup of tea with one arm can now walk from room to room holding it stably,\" he says. \n\"Another person set their post-stroke personal best in a 5K run and noticed their arm function was better when they were running.\"\nThe results of the Triceps trial are also being closely monitored by the Stroke Association, which is part-funding the research.\n\"The team are doing brain imaging and taking blood tests because we know that some stroke patients really respond well to this technology while some don't,\" said Maeva May, the charity's associate director of systems engagement.\n\"We want to understand what patients respond best and how.\"\nDr Baig says if effective, the tech could be \"quite scalable\" because it is affordable, convenient and can be easily integrated into existing rehab services.\nFor Amanda, the impact is already clear.\n\"It's about being independent again,\" she says. \"Those small things make a big difference.\"\n![A green promotional banner with black squares and rectangles forming pixels, moving in from the right. The text says: \u201cTech Decoded: The world\u2019s biggest tech news in your inbox every Monday.\u201d](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/41d3/live/348b21e0-26a8-11f0-8f57-b7237f6a66e6.png.webp)\n[Outside the UK? Sign up here](https://cloud.email.bbc.com/techdecoded-newsletter-signup).\n[Health](https://www.bbc.com/news/health)\n[Disability](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/ceynerlpwjzt)\n[Technology](https://www.bbc.com/future/tags/technology)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Park rangers 'saved' man suffering heart attack](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cpv8zxvw4leo)\n## [Report suggests sugary drinks and anxiety link](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c1d63rynpwro)\n## [Women calling for greater support with menopause](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cz0gmz34gjjo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 2 hrs ago ![Colin Lang, a man who is looking directly at the camera and smiling. "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Iran must abandon enriched uranium and not produce more, Netanyahu says\n![Reuters Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu gestures as he speaks during the Conference of Presidents of Major American Jewish Organisations, in Jerusalem,](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4cbd/live/71862300-0ac4-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Reuters\nIsraeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu says the US must require Iran to relinquish all of its enriched uranium and be barred from enriching more, as part of any nuclear deal with Tehran.\nIn a speech in Jerusalem on Sunday, he outlined several conditions he wanted, including that \"all enriched material has to leave Iran\" and that \"there should be no enrichment capability\".\nHis comments come as Iranian and US officials prepare for a second round of talks in Switzerland on Tuesday.\nIran will consider compromises to reach a nuclear deal if the US is willing to discuss lifting sanctions, Iranian deputy foreign minister Majid Takht-Ravanchi told the BBC in Tehran.\nHowever, Iran regards zero enrichment as a red line and a violation of its rights under the nuclear non-proliferation treaty.\nThe US has piled pressure on Iran to reach a deal and has built up its military capabilities in the Middle East, sending two aircraft carriers \u2013 including its largest-ever warship \u2013 to the region.\nAdditional American naval forces will protect against any potential threats in the region, US Secretary of State Marco Rubio told a news conference in Slovakia.\nHe said US President Donald Trump \"prefers diplomacy and an outcome of negotiated settlement\" over direct attacks on Iran.\nTrump's top envoy Steve Witkoff and son-in-law Jared Kushner are set to meet Iranian negotiators including Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi, who is travelling to Geneva.\n\"No-one's ever been able to do a successful deal with Iran, but we're going to try,\" Rubio said.\nUnder the [2015 nuclear deal](https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-33521655), from which Trump withdrew, Iran had agreed to limit its nuclear activities and allow international inspections in return for the lifting of crippling economic sanctions.\nAhead of the latest round of talks, Takht-Ravanchi told the BBC Tehran has offered to dilute its 60%-enriched uranium as evidence of its willingness to compromise.\nHighly enriched uranium, at near weapons-grade level, has deepened suspicions that the Islamic Republic is moving towards the development of a nuclear weapon, something it has always denied.\n\"We are ready to discuss this and other issues related to our programme if they are ready to talk about sanctions,\" Takht-Ravanchi told the BBC. He would not confirm whether this meant lifting all or some sanctions.\nHe also did not say whether Iran would consider agreeing to ship more than 400kg of highly enriched uranium out of the country.\nWashington has been pushing for a deal with Tehran, but Netanyahu said he raised \"scepticism\" with Trump during a recent meeting at the White House.\n\"I said that if a deal is to be reached, it should have several components... that we believe are important not only for the security of Israel, but for the security of the world,\" Netanyahu said.\nOne of Israel's key demands include curbing Iran's development ballistic missiles, which have been used to strike Israel.\nTehran has so far refused to discuss its ballistic missile programme with US officials. \nIsrael has also previously called for Iran to cut support for proxy groups in the Middle East, including Hamas and Hezbollah.\n## [Iran ready to discuss compromises to reach nuclear deal, minister tells BBC in {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c3w822n7827o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Is speed dating the new Tinder for Gen Z?\nHampshire speed daters told us what they think about taking love offline again.\nWith online dating apps seeing a decline in users, is old-fashioned speed dating being swiped right for Gen Zs looking for love? \n\"People don't want to waste three weeks to find out if someone isn't as funny as they seem or they don't look the way they do on their profile,\" said Lissy Cleminson, of Slow Dating - one of a number of dating firms seeing a rise in face-to-face events.\nSome under 30s singletons at an event in Hampshire told the BBC that taking love offline was a chance to get out and meet new people - in the flesh, as well as embrace something different.\nAlthough Gen Zs use online dating tools \n![A hand is about to hit a bright yellow bell. It was a smiley face on it. There is a ring on the thumb.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ef9b/live/0a055340-e7b6-11ef-ae7d-97b156abf29f.jpg.webp)\nAll participants from a speed dating event in Winchester said they'd do it again\nSpeed dating is a matchmaking event that enables eligible singles to meet potential partners in a very short space of time.\n\"Traditionally speed dating is three minutes,\" Ms Cleminson explained.\n\"However, we give you four minutes, hence the name Slow Dating.\" \nShe said the firm had seen a 12% increase in planned events every year for the past four years.\nAlmost five million people used online dating services in May, according to a new report from \nBut the four most popular dating apps in the UK - Tinder, Hinge, Bumble and Grindr - all lost UK users between May 2023 and May 2024, the report shows.\nTinder users dropped by 23%, Bumble users fell by 26% and Hinge, which has the youngest user base, experienced a 9% drop. \nMariko Visserman, a psychology lecturer at the University of Sussex who studies romantic relationships, said people were getting dating \"burnout\". \n\"People have choice overload - so they see a lot of options swiping through and I think it takes a lot of energy to actually keep swiping,\" he said.\n\"And we even know from research that what people think they want in a partner is not very predictive... of who they actually end up matching with.\"\n![A man and woman are sitting across from each other at a speed dating event. and find out what how they smell when they meet in person\n## 'Really efficient'\nTom, from Portsmouth, said he had attended speed dating events before and would recommend them to anyone thinking of giving them a go.\n\"It's just a great way of meeting people in general and connecting with new people,\" he said.\n\"You just have to be yourself and let things happen.\"\nSam, from Winchester, said the world of modern dating could be \"very tiresome\".\nOf speed dating he said: \"You don't have to spend ages talking to someone to get a vibe.\n\"You get that right balance of a few minutes of getting to know someone, who that person is, their personality and their mannerisms,\" she added. \nMiss Visserman said: \"Speed dating is a really efficient way of getting to meet someone face to face.\n\"So try and take your online dating offline as quickly as possible\".\n\"Online dating can be great, it's just up to us how we use it\".\n_You can follow_ _on_ _,__, or_ _._\nMore from BBC {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c5y48j0exlgo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# An abduction, a Bitcoin demand and a TV icon - why Nancy Guthrie's case has gripped the US\n![Getty Images A woman in a teal sweatsuit bends over and lays flowers at a memorial for Nancy Guthrie ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d1dd/live/3928ece0-0a79-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nBefore the sun rose over a barren desert landscape, YouTuber Jimmy Williams watched as hundreds of subscribers took to his online chat room.\nA laptop balanced precariously on his knees, his iPhone framed by a ring light, he spends several hours a day dissecting the details of Nancy Guthrie's sudden disappearance with his rapidly growing audience of fellow true-crime enthusiasts.\nNancy Guthrie disappeared from her home in Tucson, Arizona, and authorities believe she was taken against her will in the early hours of 1 February.\nWilliams is just one of dozens of curious observers and amateur sleuths who have descended on the area.\nThe influx of spectators has turned a quiet mid-size city, popular with retirees, into a global media spectacle.\nPolice have yet to identify any suspects in her abduction, and her daughter \u2013 the Today show presenter Savannah Guthrie \u2013 has repeatedly begged the public for help in finding her mother.\nA reassuring face on morning television in the US, Savannah Guthrie now finds herself at the same intersection of hope and dread that has defined many of her interviewees.\nThis time, however, the roles have been reversed, and instead of asking questions she is the one awaiting answers.\nBreakfast television affords its presenters a special sense of intimacy with their audience, with segments often focusing on human-interest stories.\nSavannah Guthrie spent her childhood in Tucson and in November she even invited people into her mum's home for a segment on the Today show.\nKelly Himes lives several miles from Nancy Guthrie's home in the shadow of the Catalina Mountains. Like a growing number of Tucson residents, she felt compelled to stop and say a prayer alongside the growing array of flowers at the end of her driveway.\n\"Nancy's the same age as my mum, the same height as my mum, and she's got the same hair,\" Himes tells the BBC. \"She lives alone just like my mum does. This is someone who could be anybody's mother.\"\nSavannah Guthrie's fame has proved to be a double-edged sword. As well as drawing an unprecedented amount of attention and resources to the search for her mother, it has also inspired feverish speculation and given rise to a string of false leads.\n![Getty Images Camera crews setup near Nancy Guthrie's home, the sky is dark, with with the sun nearly set](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/fd51/live/d143d290-092c-11f1-9b8e-358033f9eafe.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThe case has turned the quiet mid-size city of Tucson into a global media spectacle\nTwo weeks after Nancy Guthrie's disappearance, investigators revealed they had found several gloves, with the closest discovered about two miles from her home.\nThe sheriff's department also said media reports that a glove had been found inside her home or on the property were inaccurate.\nTo Williams' growing audience, speculation over the case only heightens its intrigue.\nSince Nancy Guthrie's disappearance, Williams' YouTube channel Dollyvision has gained more than 8,000 new subscribers.\nInterest in Nancy's disappearance is \"massive\", he says.\n\"You expect kidnappings to involve cash in a bag dropped off somewhere remote, and then the police turn up and arrest everyone. This is different. This is like a 'new age' of kidnapping.\"\nThe days of ransom demands made up of letters cut from the pages of a magazine seem a thing of the past.\nSecure servers, secret IP addresses and untraceable Bitcoin accounts have changed the face of one of the world's oldest crimes - but a former FBI hostage negotiator, Chip Massey, says he believes Nancy Guthrie's kidnappers would soon be caught.\nReferring to the video images of a masked man on her doorstep shortly before she disappeared, he said somebody, somewhere, would recognise the suspect based either on their behaviour or their body language.\n\"The attention this case has generated is unprecedented,\" he says, \"and while a mask can conceal a face, it can't conceal a person.\"\n![Getty Images A banner hung on the gate outside a local NBC affiliate shows a picture of Nancy Guthrie and reads: Bring Her Home](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/7738/live/3fb23d20-092d-11f1-9b8e-358033f9eafe.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nSavannah Guthrie has repeatedly begged the public for help in finding her mother\nMissing person flyers featuring pictures of Nancy's smiling face, combined with motorway billboards promoting the FBI's tip line have made this case impossible to ignore, even for those who don't watch the news. \nThe White House has said the FBI would be giving the investigation its \"full resources\".\n\"So many people now seem to be engaged with this search that it is going to very hard for this individual to function,\" says Massey. \n\"That sort of scrutiny makes it impossible for a suspect to move and creates a sense of the world 'closing in' on them. That's when they start to make mistakes.\"\nYellow ribbons now adorn the trees around Nancy Guthrie's home - but as days pass with no word of her whereabouts, the hope of an emotional homecoming is rapidly giving way to a deepening sense of dread.\n[United States](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cx1m7zg01xyt)\n[Arizona](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cx1m7zg0wm7t)\n* * *\nRelated\n## ['It's never too late': Savannah Guthrie's new plea for mother's release as FBI analyses glove](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c5yrv2g16e2o)\n## [Iran must abandon enriched uranium and not produce more, Netanyahu says](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cly191kz1lpo)\n## [What we know about the abduction of Savannah Guthrie's mother ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cdex1nlrj07o)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 1 day ago ![An FBI agent, with his back to the camera stands in front of a vehicle being towed. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c4gjx1j1nkjo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Trump has big plans for Venezuela's oil but are they feasible?\n![AFP via Getty Images A man walks past a mural depicting an oil pumpjack, or 'nodding donkey' on a Venezuelan flag in the country's capital Caracas](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/a1d6/live/398af810-065d-11f1-ad0b-39fef3bf1fc8.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nVenezuela has the world's largest oil reserves, but the industry has been starved of investment\nAfter US President Donald Trump oversaw the seizure of his Venezuelan counterpart Nicol\u00e1s Maduro last month, he vowed to tap the country's oil reserves \u2013 the world's largest.\nBut for the US oil firms that Trump wants to go into Venezuela, the question is a simple one - do the numbers add up?\nIn Trump's eyes, the raid that toppled President Maduro is a big business opportunity for the US oil sector, especially now that Venezuelan lawmakers have passed a bill allowing greater foreign investment in the sector.\n\"We're going to be extracting numbers in terms of oil like few people have seen,\" Trump said at a news conference in mid-January, after a meeting with energy bosses at the White House.\nWilliam Jackson, chief emerging markets economist at Capital Economics, says the US president's aim is to \"revive Venezuela's oil sector and use that energy to increase supply and reduce costs to the consumer, possibly providing a source of revenue for a more friendly Venezuelan government to rebuild the economy after years of mismanagement\".\nFor US energy companies, however, there are huge practical difficulties to be overcome. Venezuela's state-owned oil company, PDVSA, is a shadow of its former self.\nThe governments of Maduro and his predecessor, Hugo Ch\u00e1vez milked the firm for all it was worth, and used the money to finance social spending on housing, healthcare and transport.\nBut they failed to invest in maintaining oil production levels, which have plummeted in recent years \u2013 partly, but not solely, because of US sanctions, which could now be revised.\n\"In Venezuela, you're dealing with equipment that's been degraded by many years of neglect,\" says Jackson. \"Ten to 15 years ago, Venezuela was producing 1.5 million barrels a day more than it does today.\"\nMonica de Bolle, senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics, agrees that PDVSA is in a parlous state.\n\"A lot of things have to be scrapped completely and rebuilt from the ground up,\" she tells the BBC. \"In fact, if political constraints did not matter, the best thing to do would be to scrap PDVSA, but that isn't going to happen.\n\"It's a big nationalist symbol, it's attached to sovereignty. Would the Venezuelans be willing to do whatever the US says and roll over? I don't think so.\"\n![AFP via Getty Images Venezuelan oil facilities out at sea](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/66ec/live/af02af70-065d-11f1-ad0b-39fef3bf1fc8.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nVenezuela's oil facilities have been allowed to become rundown\nTrump has asked US oil firms to spend at least $100bn (\u00a375bn) on restoring Venezuela's battered infrastructure \u2013 an absolute necessity before his plan to ramp up sales can be realised.\nOfficially, Venezuela has 300 billion barrels of oil reserves \u2013 yet in 2023, it exported just 211.6 million barrels of oil, worth about $4bn. Compare that to second-placed Saudi Arabia, which has 267 billion barrels of reserves, but had exports worth $181bn in the same time period.\nSo on paper at least, there is room for improvement. However, Jackson says there are doubts over the true size of Venezuela's oil reserves.\nDuring the Ch\u00e1vez presidency, Venezuela reclassified its reserves. Previously, there were thought to be just 80 billion barrels of extractable oil, but by 2011, its reported figure had nearly quadrupled. That statistical change was made possible by high oil prices at the time, which allowed previously unviable projects to look feasible.\n\"There was a big step \u2013 jump \u2013 that people have questioned,\" Jackson says. \"But now the world is awash with oil and it's not clear that the same calculations still apply.\"\nWhen Ch\u00e1vez became Venezuela's president in 1999, oil prices were climbing. In the early 2010s, a barrel would often fetch about $100, providing the government with plenty of money to pour into social programmes. But with current prices around the $65 mark, the country looks less like a reasonable investment.\nVenezuela's oil is also of poorer quality than its Saudi equivalent. Its sour, heavy crude is difficult to extract and refine, while its high sulphur content makes it corrosive to pipelines. A resurgence of Venezuela's industry could pose problems for Canada, which produces similarly viscous oil and exports much of it to the US, but analysts reckon the risk is minor.\nAccording to research by Capital Economics, Canadian oil should remain competitively priced, even if Venezuelan production increases.\nIn the meantime, Venezuela's economic crisis has led to the exodus of nearly eight million people who have fled in search of a better life.\nThis includes expertise vital to keeping the oil pumps working: with the skilled engineers formerly on the PDVSA payroll now plying their trade elsewhere, the system limps on with a skeleton staff.\nThomas Watters, managing director and sector lead for oil and gas at research firm S&P Global Ratings, says US firms have the ability to repair Venezuela's infrastructure, but it has to make economic sense.\n\"At the end of the day, oil and gas companies have to deliver value to shareholders,\" he says. \"They have very good managers. You can build anything, as long as you can pay for it.\n\"But you need an oil price that makes that worthwhile. Unless you can generate sufficient money to justify that, it's very difficult to see the industry coming back.\"\n![Monica de Bolle Monica de Bolle, senior fellow at the Peterson Institute for International Economics, smiles at the camera](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/eee9/live/20efe080-065e-11f1-ad0b-39fef3bf1fc8.jpg.webp)Monica de Bolle\nMonica de Bolle says that Venezuela's state oil firm needs to be rebuilt\nBesides, US oil firms have been bitten by Venezuela once before. In 2007, major US companies including ExxonMobil and ConocoPhillips had their assets seized when they refused to allow PDVSA to take majority control.\nThey went to the international courts and were awarded huge sums in damages \u2013 $8.3bn in the case of ConocoPhillips \u2013 which have never been paid.\nGiven that the current Venezuelan regime continues largely intact, with former Vice-President Delcy Rodr\u00edguez as interim leader, it will take a lot to dispel fears of renewed expropriation.\nMoreover, US Energy Secretary Chris Wright has said the Trump administration has no plans to offer security guarantees to oil companies in Venezuela \u2013 a worrying omission in a country where state-sanctioned paramilitary groups known as \"colectivos\" often operate as criminal gangs.\nWithout bigger government incentives, oil firms will be reluctant to take what could be an expensive plunge. Small wonder, then, that ExxonMobil boss Darren Woods has called Venezuela \"uninvestable\" in its current state.\nTellingly, Trump did not come back with an offer of sweeteners to promote investment. Instead, he threatened to block ExxonMobil investment in Venezuela.\nThe policy is \"all stick, no carrot\", says de Bolle of the Peterson Institute. \"And it doesn't seem like they understand that they do need carrots.\"\n![AFP via Getty Images Venezuelan policemen stand in front of a protest in Caracas over low wages](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/f9a1/live/e9d101b0-065d-11f1-ad0b-39fef3bf1fc8.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nUS oil firms are also said to be concerned about the ongoing security situation in Venezuela\nIn de Bolle's view, the Trump administration has an \"imperialist vision\" of Latin America that leads it to see the region's resources as its property. For her, private oil firms' aversion to Venezuela is a welcome barrier to that kind of resource grab.\n\"This is a time when you think, 'Thank God the US doesn't have a state-owned oil company,'\" she says. \"They need the private sector, but for the moment, the private sector isn't budging. And what company in their right mind is going to put money into Venezuela?\"\nBut if Venezuela's oil output does eventually surge, could it bring global oil prices down? Analysts refuse to be drawn.\n\"It depends on the scale at which it happens,\" says Jackson of Capital Economics.\n\"The situation is very fluid, very opaque, and there's a big geopolitical angle. We're in the early stages where Venezuelan production is concerned.\"\nRead more global business stories\n[US economy](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c1038wnxypvt)\n[World of Business](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c4gmdg9ne38t)\n[International Business](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c4nrdv135zet)\n[Venezuela](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cg41ylwvwgxt)\n[Venezuela {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cddn002g6qzo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Trump's new world order has become real and Europe is having to adjust fast\n![BBC A European Union flag and a US' national flag next to an image of Trump ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/c404/live/d2f08810-08f3-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)BBC\nDowntown Munich is best-known for chic shops and flashy fast cars but right now its streets are bedecked with posters advertising next generation drones.\n\"Europe's security under construction\" boasts the slogan on an eye-catching set of sleek black-and-white photographs, festooned across a scaffolding-clad church on one of this town's best known pedestrian boulevards.\nSuch an unapologetic public display of military muscle would have been unimaginable here just a few years ago, but the world outside Germany is changing fast, and taking this country with it.\nThe southern region of Bavaria has become Germany's leading defence technology hub, focusing on AI, drones and aerospace.\n![\"Europe's security under construction\" boasts the slogan on a set of sleek black-and-white photographs which are festooned across a scaffolding-clad church with people walking in the foreground\n\n](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/a2b0/live/d65ff010-0ac6-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)\nAdvertisements such as these that say \"Europe's security under construction\" would have been unthinkable in Germany not long ago\nPeople here, like most other Europeans, say they feel increasingly exposed - squeezed between an expansionist Russia and an economically aggressive China to the east, and an increasingly unpredictable, former best pal, the United States, to the west.\nAccording to a recent Eurobarometer poll, more than two-thirds of Europeans (68%) feel their country is under threat.\nThis autumn, Germany's Federal Office of Civil Protection and Disaster Assistance warned for the first time since the Cold War that war is no longer \"unlikely\". While emphasising that this is a safe country, it also recommends that Germans keep food supplies of three to ten days at home. Just in case.\nGermany is the number one single donor of military and other aid to Ukraine, now that the US has halted any new direct aid. Opinion polls suggest voters here want to feel better protected at home too.\n![AFP via Getty Images Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky \\(L\\) and German Chancellor Friedrich Merz shake hands prior bilateral talks during the 62nd Munich Security Conference ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/c2a6/live/035ebd30-08fb-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nGermany is now the single biggest giver of aid to Ukraine\nThe question for this country, along with others in Europe is whether traditional alliances with the US, in Nato and the EU can suffice, or whether they should be diversifying into ad-hoc coalitions alongside other like-minded nations such as Australia, South Korea and Japan?\n## Precarious relations\nBy 2029, the German defence budget will be higher than the UK and French equivalents combined, Nato's Secretary General Mark Rutte pointed out to me.\nHe described the \u20ac150bn they say they will spend on defence as \"a staggering amount\".\nIt's something the United States notices and appreciates, he said. Donald Trump is far from the first US president to insist that Europe do more for its own security, though his tone has been noticeably more threatening than that of his predecessors.\nThe precarious state of transatlantic relations was the main focus of the Munich Security Conference (MSC) this weekend. It's the world's biggest annual defence meeting, bringing leaders, security experts and defence industries together.\n![Katya Adler on the left and Mark Rutte on the right with a branded MSC board behind them.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/360d/live/d491a010-0ac3-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)\nMark Rutte (pictured speaking to Katya Adler), described the E150bn Germany will spend on defence \"a staggering amount\"\nWhile it's easy to dismiss speech-heavy get-togethers like this as wind-baggy talking shops, in the turbulent times we live in, they can make a difference - especially the informal private huddles between global decision makers, far away from the glare of the cameras.\nThe most eagerly - and for some the most anxiously - anticipated speech at this year's conference was that of the US Secretary of State, Marco Rubio, who represented the Trump administration here.\nEuropean leaders and top diplomats were seriously on the edge of their seats. But why was a simple 30-minute address given so much importance?\nIt's because Europe-US relations have never been so frayed as they are now, over the last 80 years since World War Two. And this isn't a bust-up between buddies that will easily blow over.\n## Denmark still furious\nIn the just over 12 months since Trump returned to the White House, he has at times insulted and undermined European leaders, slapped big tariffs on their exports, and most shockingly of all to his allies in Nato, threatened Danish sovereignty over its territory, Greenland, refusing for a while to rule out taking the island by force.\nSpeaking at the MSC on Saturday, Denmark's still clearly furious prime minister, Mette Frederiksen, said Trump's designs on Greenland remained \"the same\" despite ongoing trilateral talks between representatives of Greenland, the US and Denmark.\nTrump has ruled out taking Greenland by military force for now, and he's backed away (for the moment, at least) from slapping economic sanctions on allies, including the UK, France and Germany, that were getting in the way of the US acquiring the Arctic island. But transatlantic trust was severely damaged.\nEuropean powers see in Trump a truly transactional president who thinks nothing of leveraging security or economic relations with his closest allies to get what he wants. Just before being re-elected president, for example, he told Europeans that the US would not protect nations that didn't pay their way on defence.\nIt is true though, that Europe has been coasting on America's security blanket for decades. Critics in the US argue that European nations have been able to run generous welfare states for decades while Washington picked up the tab for security spending.\n![AFP via Getty Images German Chancellor Friedrich Merz \\(R\\) and US Secretary of State Marco Rubio attend a bilateral meeting on the sidelines of the Munich Security Conference \n](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/80c2/live/f9f8bbb0-08fa-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)AFP via Getty Images\nThe speech by US Secretary of State, Marco Rubio (left) was the most eagerly anticipated at the conference\nGermany's defence minister, Boris Pistorius, told me on Saturday: \"We got used to the strong support from the US; we got used to our comfort zone in which we used to live. This time is over, definitely over,\" he said. \"Washington was right.\"\nBut the crisis over Greenland and other actions by the Trump administration - such as when it temporarily paused intelligence-sharing with Ukrainian forces last March, leaving them blind on the battlefield, in order to pressure Kyiv into engaging in peace talks with Moscow - have left deep scars and a troubling sense of transatlantic wariness.\nHence the trepidation in Munich before Rubio took the stage.\nIn the end, his words were laced with a sense of historic kinship. \"We want Europe to be strong,\" he said. \"The two great wars of the last century serve for us as a constant reminder that ultimately our destiny is, and will always be, intertwined with yours.\"\n## A loyal speech\nI found it telling that so many European politicians and politicians in the audience jumped on the warmth of his words, rising to their feet to applaud the US Secretary of State. They were clearly relieved he hadn't threatened or berated Europe as the US Vice President JD Vance famously did at last year's MSC.\nBut for those listening closely, Rubio's speech was loyal to themes close to the heart of the Trump administration and hard for many European leaders to swallow: anti climate action, sceptical of globalisation, multilateralism, migration and pro the building of a new era of Christian western civilisation.\nRubio was clear: the US wasn't interested in allies clinging to the old status quo. It wanted to forge a new path, ideally alongside Europe, but only if it shared the same values.\nThis US offer of close partnership was conditional and absent of a sense of compromise.\n\"A bit like a (psychologically) abusive partner,\" said one European diplomat, speaking candidly on condition of anonymity. \"He reminded Europe how wonderful the (transatlantic) relationship used to be, but he then switched to coercion: If you want things to be good between us in the future, you have to do as I say!\"\nAnother diplomat pointed out that, while talking of shared values, it was telling, he said, that of all the European countries that Rubio could have gone to after making his speech in Germany, he chose to visit Slovakia and Hungary before heading back to the US.\nThey are viewed by Brussels as two of the EU's most problematic members, both with Eurosceptic nationalist prime ministers who oppose sending military aid to Ukraine and who are tough on migration.\n## A fragile new relationship\nRubio's softer tone also divided European leaders who had recently spoken as one, in defence of Denmark, at the height of the Greenland crisis last month.\nThe President of the European Commission, Ursula von der Leyen, underlined a now fragile relationship with the US, despite Rubio's gentler rhetoric. \"Some lines have been crossed that cannot be uncrossed anymore,\" she said. \"Europeans have suffered shock therapy.\"\nBut will some countries in Europe grasp what warmness there was in Rubio's speech as an excuse not to rush to boost defence spending as promised? The coffers of most European governments are over stretched already and their voters tend to prioritise cost-of-living concerns over defence budgets.\n![Reuters British Prime Minister Keir Starmer takes part in a panel discussion with President of the European Commission Ursula von der Leyen during the Munich Security Conference. He is on the left and she is on the right, both seated.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4aa5/live/879eb190-0ac7-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Reuters\n\"Some lines have been crossed that cannot be uncrossed anymore,\" according to the President of the European Commission, Ursula von der Leyen\nRachel Ellehuus, Director-General of defence think-tank RUSI told me she sees a rift opening up across the continent.\nOne the one hand, you have the Nordic and Baltic nations that are geographically close to Russia, and also Germany and the Netherlands, which are all big defence spenders, she says, whereas in southern Europe, there's Spain, for example, that is absolutely unapologetic about refusing to increase defence budgets to the levels demanded by Donald Trump.\nFrance and Britain are both verbally committed to boosting defence spending, says Ellehuus, but are still looking for a \"political band aid\" to help them explain to voters the trade-offs that will involve - higher taxes, less welfare or more borrowing.\n\"Europeans need to get to work yesterday and to focus,\" she says. \"They have 5-10 years to stand on their own two feet in terms of conventional defence capabilities.\"\nLast week, U.S. Undersecretary of Defence Elbridge Colby couldn't have been more stark in his messaging at a meeting of Nato defence ministers he attended in Brussels: Europe was no longer a US priority, the Indo-Pacific was.\n\"Under President Trump's leadership, we are reprioritising the defence of our homeland and the protection of our interests in our Hemisphere,\" he said.\nWhile he emphasised that the US remained committed to Nato's mutual defence clause, where an attack against one member is viewed as an attack against all, Colby insisted the US would be reducing its capabilities in Europe, becoming \"a more limited and focused\" presence.\nEurope had to become a partner, rather than a dependent, he said, calling for a new \"Nato 3.0\". The old world order with the West at its core, has faded but the MSC this weekend made clear that what comes next for Europe and the US is still very much up in the air.\nMarco Rubio called for a new century of western civilisation, Elbridge Colby wants a re-vamped Nato, while the UK's prime minister appealed in Munich for the western alliance to be re-made.\n## Starmer's nuanced approach\nIn stark contrast to Marco Rubio's insistence on greater national sovereignty, Sir Keir Starmer spoke in favour of greater integration between the UK and Europe on defence - to cut re-armament costs, though, he emphasised this did not mean the UK turning its back on the United States.\nSophia Gaston, national security expert at Kings College London, told me that in Munich, Starmer was able to better articulate the nuance of Britain's strategic outlook.\n\"Other allies in Europe may be more willing to speak of divergence from Washington\", she says, \"but for Britain it remains a strategic imperative to triangulate within the Transatlantic relationship. There will also be times when Britain will have to make hard choices, and Starmer appeared more confident in confronting that reality.\n\"The key is to have a really strong grasp of the national interest and our instruments of power and influence. This requires a much more competitive approach that has not always felt natural to Britain, which has typically pursued much of its diplomacy in elegant, consensus-focused and invisible ways.\"\n![PA Media Keir Starmer in a dark suit, blue tie and holding a microphone in his left hand at the Munich conference ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4bc2/live/41f5a2b0-0ac8-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)PA Media\nKeir Starmer spoke in favour of greater integration between the UK and Europe on defence\nIn these fast-moving, unpredictable times, Europe's leaders are increasingly turning to a la carte coalitions, alongside traditional organisations like Nato or the EU, which are larger and therefore often slower to react. These groupings also include non-European nations.\nTake, for example, the so-called Coalition of the Willing group of countries, led by the UK and France and formed to secure Ukrainian sovereignty in the case of an eventual peace deal with Russia. Turkey has attended coalition meetings, as have New Zealand and Australia.\nMore from InDepth\nCanada is increasingly working alongside Nordic and Baltic nations that share geopolitical challenges and common values, they say, and want to promote stability and deterrence from the edges of the Baltic Sea, through the Nordic and Baltic states, through the North Atlantic, Greenland and across the Canadian arctic.\nOne European policy-maker who asked not to be named joked to me that Canada was becoming \"more and more European by the day\". Japan and South Korea were increasingly seen as part of the \"like-minded family\" too, he said.\n## Not just defence\nThese ad hoc coalitions are not restricted to defence alone. France's President Macron has long called for Europe to increase what he calls its strategic autonomy, in traditional security terms but also energy security, supply chains and new technologies. In Munich he recommended Europe \"de-risk\" from all outside powers.\nThis weekend even saw the president of the European Commission admitting that if the EU failed to move fast enough on improving competitiveness, \"a group of its member states would have to move forward alone\".\nAttempts by smaller pockets of European powers to work together to advance European strategic independence are by no means always successful. Take the current row between France and Germany over their joint attempt to come up with the next generation of fighter jet with the Future Combat Air System (FCAS).\nYou could also say that for all the talk of building European independence, this weekend's conference was also a reminder of just how dependent Europe remains on US security support - from its nuclear umbrella to intelligence sharing and command and control structures. It was also a reminder of how far Europe trails the US in high tech innovation.\nBut more than short-term mechanisms designed to 'survive' Donald Trump, the changes we are witnessing in Europe, including closer alliances outside the continent, are liable to be longer lasting. The world now seems to dance to the tune of big power politics. And even slow-moving Europe is having to adapt.\n_Top picture credit: AFP / Getty Images_\n![Thin, lobster red banner with white text saying \u2018InDepth newsletter\u2019. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c74754j7qwgo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Obama addresses racist video shared by Trump depicting him as an ape\n![Getty Images Obama seen on the campaign trail in November 2025](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/dfb9/live/6d4a11c0-09d9-11f1-bfd9-a1813d3ca61e.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nObama seen on the campaign trail in November 2025\nFormer US President Barack Obama has indirectly addressed a racist video posted on President Donald Trump's social media, telling a podcast host that the \"shame\" and \"decorum\" that once guided public officials is now lost.\nThe offensive video included a clip depicting Obama and his wife Michelle as apes, which drew widespread criticism from Democrats and Republicans. \nThe White House initially defended the video, calling backlash \"fake outrage\". The post was later blamed on a staff member and deleted.\nObama spoke to liberal podcaster Brian Tyler Cohen, who asked the first black US president about the tone of political discourse. Cohen cited Trump's post among several recent controversies. \nThe clip - set to the song The Lion Sleeps Tonight - was included at the end of a video Trump's Truth Social account shared containing unfounded claims about voter fraud in the 2020 election. \nThe post led to outrage from politicians, including from senior members of Trump's Republican party.\nSenator Tim Scott - the only black Republican senator - described it as \"the most racist thing I've seen out of this White House\".\nThe clip recalls racist caricatures comparing black people to monkeys, and appears to have been taken from an X post shared by conservative meme creator Xerias in October.\nTrump has told reporters that he \"didn't see\" the part of the video that showed the Obamas.\n\"I didn't make a mistake,\" he told reporters when asked whether he planned to apologise.\nThe 47-minute podcast featuring Obama was released on Saturday. The episode begins with the host asking him to comment on US \"discourse\", which he says \"has devolved to a level of cruelty that we haven't seen before\".\nCohen notes claims by the White House that the \"victims\" of Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) are \"domestic terrorists\", and adds \"just days ago, Donald Trump put a picture of you, your face, on an ape's body\".\nObama responds by saying: \"It's important to recognise that the majority of the American people find this behaviour deeply troubling.\n\"It is true that it gets attention. It's true that it's a distraction.\"\nBut Obama said that while travelling around the US, he found himself meeting people who \"still believe in decency, courtesy, kindness\".\n\"There's this sort of clown show that's happening in social media and on television,\" he continued. \n\"And what is true is that there doesn't seem to be any shame about this among people who used to feel like you had to have some sort of decorum and a sense of propriety and respect for the office, right? \n\"That's been lost.\"\nHe did not mention Trump by name in his response.\nDuring the interview, Obama spoke about a range of issues. with both stars indicating a close bond while promoting the film.\nWuthering Heights is the latest in a string of films where it's been suggested romance rumours were used as promo: think Sydney Sweeney and Glen Powell in Anyone But You, and Lady Gaga and Bradley Cooper in A Star is Born.\nRobbie and Elordi may have successfully got us talking, but some fans have been left less than impressed with their behaviour, with one telling BBC News it gave her \"the ick\", and another calling the display \"weird and tacky\".\nSome also suspect the actors' gushing words about each other may be just another cog in the film's PR machine, especially given Robbie's husband, Tom Ackerley, is one of its producers.\nSo have the stars' supposedly intimate moments been designed to generate romance rumours (and social media buzz) around \n## A 'mutual obsession'\n![Warner Bros Jacob Elordi and Margot Robbie from a scene in the film](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/1d6c/live/1bfd9ab0-074c-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Warner Bros\nLet's start with what exactly Robbie and Elordi have said and done.\nThroughout the press tour for Emerald Fennell's hotly anticipated film, the two Aussie A-listers have been dishing out viral quotes.\nIn January, \nElsewhere, Robbie \nThen there was Elordi's romantic gesture for Robbie during filming on Valentine's Day.\n\"You made my day and, as Heathcliff, filled my room with roses and it was so cute,\" Robbie told Elordi during a conversation with her co-star published in \n\"I remember thinking on Valentine's Day, 'Oh he's probably a very good boyfriend, 'cause there's a lot of thoughtfulness in this',\" she continued.\nMargot Robbie, Jacob Elordi and Charli XCX on Wuthering Heights\nBut Elordi seemed less on-message \nSpeaking on the red carpet at the London premiere, he looked a bit startled and paused for a period, before answering: \"Yes. 29-year-old actress and producer, agrees. \"The trouble is, they're trying to romanticise a dark and toxic story, so that is setting the audience up for a failure,\" she says.\n\"They might walk into this film thinking it's a light and fluffy romantic film, as they've seen it portrayed by the stars, when it's actually a deeply troubling story.\"\nWhile manufactured romance rumours have always been rife in the celebrity world, Ashlee-Rose says the buzz around Elordi and Robbie has \"got people's back up\" because \"we know they're not a couple\".\n\"This is a married woman with a child, so it comes across as very fake,\" she tells BBC News.\n![Warner Bros Margot Robbie and Jacob {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c4g5380xwqjo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Ukraine's ex-energy minister detained while attempting to leave country\n![Reuters German Galushchenko, with grey hair, beard, and glasses with a neutral expression. He is standing next to Volodymyr Zelensky. Both men are wearing black jackets. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/3d97/live/25367f30-0a61-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)Reuters\nGerman Galushchenko (left) was among several figures in Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky's government implicated in alleged corruption\nA former energy minister in Ukraine has been detained while attempting to leave the country, authorities have said.\nGerman Galushchenko, who was forced out of his government role last year after being among officials named in a corruption scandal, was reportedly apprehended while on a train leaving Ukraine. It is unclear where he intended to travel to.\nHe was one of several government figures implicated in an alleged $100m (\u00a375m) embezzlement scheme in November.\nThe scandal threatened to engulf Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky's administration, which swept to power before Russia's full-scale invasion on the promise to root out corruption.\nZelensky's chief-of-staff Andriy Yermak - his closest adviser throughout the war - resigned after his home was searched. Neither the president nor Yermak have been accused of any wrongdoing.\nThe scandal has, however, increased pressure from the US to hold elections, which have been suspended since the start of the war in 2022 due to provisions in Ukraine's constitution.\nThe National Anti-Corruption Bureau of Ukraine (Nabu) said in a statement on Sunday that its detectives had detained the former energy minister \"while crossing the state border\", as part of the Midas case.\nIt did not name Galushchenko explicitly, but he has been named by several prominent Ukrainian media organisations.\nGalushchenko was briefly justice minister, a post he held when he was told by Zelensky to resign in November, having served as energy minister for three years prior to that. His successor, Svitlana Hrynchuk, also resigned after being implicated in the scandal.\nNabu gave no further details on Galuschenko's detention, but said it would provide updates in due course.\nRadio Free Europe reports that he was being transported to Kyiv for further questioning, after border guards were told to alert the authorities if he attempted to flee.\nA massive anti-corruption probe, dubbed Operation Midas, was the culmination of 15 months of investigating, Nabu and Ukraine's other anti-corruption body, the Specialised Anti-Corruption Prosecutor's Office (Sap), said.\nThey accused several people of orchestrating a scheme to embezzle money in Ukraine's energy sector, including at the national nuclear opertor Energoatom.\nGaluschenko was among those alleged to have systematically collected kickbacks from Enerhoatom contractors worth between 10% and 15% of contract values.\nThe anti-corruption bodies also said the huge sums had been laundered in the scheme and published photographs of bags full of cash. The funds were then transferred outside Ukraine, including to Russia, Nabu said.\nGalushchenko previously said he would defend himself against the accusations.\nFormer Deputy Prime Minister Oleksiy Chernyshov was arrested in November on suspicion of \"illicit enrichment\", having already been charged with abuse of office. \nTimur Mindich, a businessman and a co-owner of Zelensky's former TV studio Kvartal95, reportedly fled the country after he was flagged as a suspect. \nThat the figures were accused of enriching themselves from Ukraine's energy sector caused particular outrage domestically, as the scandal broke as Russia stepped up attacks on Ukraine's energy infrastructure ahead of a bitterly cold winter.\nThe former Soviet Union member has long been plagued by issues of corruption, which have continued despite Nabu and Sap operating for a decade.\nCracking down on corruption is seen as a key requirement for Ukraine joining the EU.\n[War in Ukraine](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c1vw6q14rzqt)\n[Volodymyr Zelensky](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c4vvr0ergrzt)\n[Ukraine](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cx1m7zg0gzdt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [A day in the life of British soldiers on NATO manoeuvres](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/czd84d5mj88o)\n## [The war dead pictured on banned Ukrainian athlete's helmet](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cnv63q1vpv1o)\n## [Russian strike kills 3 Ukrainian toddlers and father, with pregnant mother injured](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/crm820dp0yro)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 6 days ago ![Yana Kozah is standing in front of a red brick wall. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cm289we7jzdo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# What is the dart frog toxin allegedly used to kill Alexei Navalny?\n![Getty Images An Anthony's poison arrow frog perched on a leaf. Its skin is red with white stripes](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/7fc2/live/a4c47040-0a98-11f1-9640-7f85e4d0c189.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nEpibatidine, which can be secreted by Anthony's poison arrow frog onto its skin, was blamed for Alexei Navalny's death by five European nations\nRussian opposition leader Alexei Navalny was killed using a deadly toxin found in poison dart frogs in South America, the UK and some of its European allies have said.\nTraces of epibatidine were found in samples from Navalny's body and highly likely resulted in his death in a Siberian penal colony two years ago, the UK Foreign Office said.\nThe allies said only the Russian state had the \"means, motive and opportunity\" to deploy this lethal toxin.\nThe Kremlin dismissed the finding as \"an information campaign\", according to Tass news agency.\n## What is the toxin?\nEpibatidine is a natural neurotoxin isolated from the skin of the Ecuadorian poison dart frog, according to toxicology expert Jill Johnson.\nIt was \"200 times more potent\" than morphine, she told BBC Russian.\nEpibatidine can be found naturally in dart frogs in the wild in South America as well as being manufactured in a laboratory.\nDart frogs in captivity do not produce this toxin and it is not found naturally in Russia, the European allies said in their statement.\nSpecies known as Anthony's poison arrow frog and the Phantasmal poison frog are among those that secrete the toxin onto their skin.\nAlthough epibatidine has been investigated as a painkiller and for relief from painful inflammatory lung conditions, it is not used clinically because of its toxicity.\n## How does dart frog poison work?\nThis powerful chemical compound acts on nicotinic receptors in the nervous system, according to Johnson.\nBecause it overstimulates these nerve receptors, if dosed correctly, it can cause muscle twitching, paralysis, seizures, slow heart rate, respiratory failure and ultimately death, she explained.\nAlastair Hay, professor of environmental toxicology at the University of Leeds, told PA that its effects can result in breathing being blocked, and that \"any person poisoned dies from suffocation\".\nThe toxin being found in someone's blood \"suggests deliberate administration\", he added.\nEpibatidine toxicity can even be \"increased by co-administration of certain other drugs and these combinations have been researched\", Hay said.\n## How rare is the toxin?\nEpibatidine is extremely rare and found in only one geographic region and only in trace amounts, Johnson said.\nIt is understood the dart frog referred to by the UK Foreign Office and others was Anthony's poison arrow frog, a species endemic to Ecuador and Peru.\nThe frogs produce the chemical through eating the right foods to produce alkaloids - a type of organic compound - that make epibatidine and accumulate it in their skin. If the frog's diet changes, its epibatidine reserves will be depleted.\n\"Finding a wild frog in the right place, eating exactly the food needed to produce the right alkaloids, is almost impossible... almost,\" Johnson said.\n\"This is an incredibly rare method of human poisoning. The only other cases of epibatidine poisoning I know of were laboratory-based and non-fatal.\"\n## What has Russia said?\nEuropean laboratories confirmed Navalny died from the obscure poison, the allies said on Saturday.\nMoscow has previously claimed Navalny died of natural causes, though Navalny's widow Yulia Navalnaya has consistently argued her husband was \"murdered\" by poisoning.\nThe Russian embassy in London denied Moscow was involved in Navalny's death and described the announcement as \"feeble-mindedness of Western fabulists\" and \"necro-propaganda\".\nKremlin spokesperson Maria Zakharova was quoted by Russian state-run news agency Tass as saying: \"All the talks and statements are an information campaign aimed at distracting attention from the West's pressing problems.\"\nAt the time of his death, Navalny had been in jail for three years and had latterly been transferred to an Arctic penal colony.\nAccording to Russian accounts, the 47-year-old took a short walk, said he felt unwell, then collapsed and never regained consciousness.\n[Russia](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/ce1qrvlegnyt)\n[Alexei Navalny](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cpzyrklr4l8t)\n[Vladimir Putin](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cvenzmgywd4t)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Russian 'pick-up artist' accused of secretly filming women in Ghana](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c9wn5p299eko)\n## [Russia killed opposition leader Alexei Navalny using dart frog toxin, UK says](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cwyk4lz4e3eo)\n## [Russia orders block on WhatsApp in messaging app crackdown](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/clygd10pg5lo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 20 hrs ago ![Foreign Secretary Yvette Cooper speaking from a conference in Munich. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cgmlvl48202o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# What the Nigella Lawson effect will mean for the Great British Bake Off\n![Getty Images Chef Nigella Lawson poses for a portrait on June 1, 2005 in London. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/43a2/live/f36a03a0-08da-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nWhen Nigella Lawson was \nBecause who other than the queen of comfort food could step into the shoes of Dame Prue Leith, who is leaving the show \nA self-described home cook, Lawson has been on our screens for more than a quarter of a century, and her bestselling books have sold more than 12 million copies globally.\nWith the Bake Off mantle - and the announcement of a new Financial Times food column - it seems Lawson is having a moment.\nShe perhaps represents a refreshing antidote to the kind of cooks whose recipes involve complicated ingredients you have to hunt for in a deli - instead, expect to find \nLawson goes for minimum effort, maximum comfort. The milk is always full fat and the butter plentiful.\n![Nigella Lawson pictured using a fork to eat food,  and placing a tray in an oven, on her TV show](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/7abf/live/441af240-08dc-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.png.webp)\nTV critic Scott Bryan says Lawson is \"incredibly qualified\" for the Bake Off role, especially as a former judge on Australian MasterChef and on short-lived Channel 4 show called The Taste.\nBryan says the TV personality makes the viewer feel as if they're \"around her house for dinner\".\n\"You don't get a sense there are cameras,\" he continues. \"She doesn't take herself too seriously - and there's no denying the fact the innuendo possibilities are endless.\"\n## TV's moved on\nIt was through food writing Lawson became well known. After working in publishing, she moved into journalism, with her first job in food as a restaurant critic.\nHer first cookery book, How to Eat, was published in 1998, with Nigella Bites airing on Channel 4 the following year. Hundreds of shows, in the UK, Australia and the US, have followed.\nWriter Stuart Heritage, who has had the honour of cooking apple crumble for Lawson, says now \"the landscape has changed an awful lot for cookery shows\".\n\"The sort of thing that she used to do, where it would be six episodes and you'd see inside her house, set the pace for what cookery shows were for such a long time,\" he says. \"Now that sort of show has changed, and we get documentaries that are more about food than just straight-up demonstrations of recipes. \n\"TV's moved on a bit - I think this is a good way of her to find a new place within it.\"\nWhile Lawson's shows have been watched by millions, her last full series was Cook, Eat, Repeat in 2020, with a Christmas special in 2023.\nWhen Heritage cooked for her, as part of the promotion for Channel 4's The Taste, he says he found Lawson \"very full beam\".\n\"She's terrifying - but lovely as well,\" Heritage says. \"I wouldn't want to be a baker who disappoints her, but I can see her being very encouraging at the same time.\"\n![Three images of Nigella Lawson, one looking at a book, one cooking and a pile of her recipe books near a vase of dark pink flowers](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4b21/live/83625ea0-08de-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.png.webp)\nLawson was one of four children born to politician Nigel Lawson and socialite Vanessa Salmon, growing up in London before studying languages at Oxford University.\n\"She doesn't hide from her privilege, and I think that makes her more accessible,\" says Heritage. \"Her dad was chancellor of the exchequer - it's a hard line to walk, but she's also very relatable.\"\nLawson has said that growing up in their London home, she found meals \"really anxiety-provoking\" and she was brought up \"in an incredibly old-fashioned way\" where if you did not eat everything on your plate, the cold remains were given for your next meal.\nHer mother had what she's described as a \"difficult relationship with food\". Lawson was determined not to be the same, something which changed the way she saw food.\nBurrell says Lawson's view of food as pleasure put her in sharp contrast with many of her contemporaries.\nLawson appeared on our screens at the time of 1990s programmes like You Are What You Eat, in which presenter Gillian McKeith tried to help people with unhealthy diets improve their health with better nutrition. \nLawson's \"indulgence\" came in stark contrast to what writer and superfan Jessica Burrell says would now be called \"food shaming\".\n\"There's a common thread: it's that food was an enemy, or something to be wary of,\" she says. \"Nigella completely, authentically, was a significant foil to that.\"\n## 'She wants to have fun'\nWhy might Lawson be choosing to make this move now, committing herself to rainy days in the Bake Off tent and tasting 12 near-identical Garibaldi biscuits?\n\"I think she's just bored - she wants to have some fun,\" says food writer Gilly Smith, who has been writing about Nigella for more than 20 years.\n\"She's very British and one of the lovely things about Bake Off is that it's a fantastic opportunity to see what Britain is,\" continues Smith, host of podcast Cooking the Books.\nJournalist Jessica Burrell, who in her 20s \n\"It does feel like she is having a moment. And I think there's something really joyful about that, not just because of everything she represents but also because she's a 66-year-old woman.\n\"It's great to see someone her age having a career moment in such a public way - and she still feels young and sexy.\"\nLawson has said her sensual image - think close-ups licking a spoon, or Lawson floating to the stove in a dressing gown with hazy jazz playing - is something she \"can't trouble\" herself with too much.\n\"Many people, in good faith, misconstrue what I'm saying, and they think I'm trying to do a sort of come-on to the camera. And I really am not,\" she \n\"I feel I have a huge raft of female viewers, who watch the show, who completely get me\u2026 What men project onto me, I can't help.\"\n![Chef Nigella Lawson poses for a portrait on June 1, 2005 \\(Getty Images\\) and for a promotional photo for her series Nigella: At My Table in 2022](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/5efd/live/bce12570-08e9-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.png.webp)\n## 'Amazingly camp'\nBut as well as being known for her glam, aspirational image, Lawson is anything but out of reach to fans cooking in their own kitchens.\nShe happily replies on social media when people tell her they're trying out her recipes. And despite being known as the Domestic Goddess, she has said she doesn't feel she lives up to the (ironic) title of her bestselling book.\n\"When I interviewed her, she said the thing she loves most is when people share what they've made of her recipes and she gets into a conversation with them,\" says Smith.\nHer image is more than just that of a \"sexy chef\", says Burrell.\n\"She is also amazingly camp and has a really dedicated fanbase within the LGBTQ+ community and she's had amazing moments go viral,\" she says.\nBosses at Channel 4 could well be hoping Lawson's arrival will lead to a boost in viewers, for a show that's now 16 years old.\n\"She represents a kind of originality that could help revive that slightly stale franchise,\" says Burrell.\nBryan adds: \"It's reaching the point where people will apply for Bake Off who weren't born when it started. It's hard to sustain a show for that long. I think she'll give it a big burst of rejuvenation.\"\nHeritage says the only other option would have been for Bake Off to hire one of its former winners - like \n\"I think now, because it's presumably closer to the end than the beginning of Bake Off, it needs a big name to draw people back.\"\nWhether Lawson proves the magic ingredient will be put to the test when the new series hits screens in autumn.\n_Photography: BBC and Getty Images_\nMore weekend picks\n[The Great British Bake Off](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c340r9425rvt)\n[Television](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c40rjmqdwr7t)\n[Nigella Lawson](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/czp3wnv1q5et)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Molly-Mae among Celebrity Bake Off 2026 line-up](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cj6wj19yzr4o)\n## [The Wirral-born baker who is now a household name](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cvgnzp11wl9o)\n## [Nigella Lawson to replace Prue Leith on The Great British Bake Off](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cgk80pzxkexo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 7 hrs ago ![Runway expert J Alexander, creator and executive producer Tyra Banks and photographer Nigel Barker on America's Next Top Model judging panel.  ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/69e7/live/c1bd1fe0-08c6-11f1-aef6-c5b8e6ae55fa.jpg) America's Next Top Model shaped reality TV, but should it ever have been made?
