{"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cdex1nlrj07o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# What we know about the abduction of Savannah Guthrie's mother \nWatch: What's happened since Savannah Guthrie's mother disappeared?\nThe mystery surrounding the disappearance of the 84-year-old mother of NBC News anchor Savannah Guthrie has deepened.\nNancy Guthrie disappeared in the middle of night from her home in Tucson, Arizona, on 31 January and authorities believe she was taken against her will.\nPolice in Arizona detained a man in connection with the suspected abduction on 10 February, before releasing him hours later. \nOfficers also searched a nearby home on 13 February and questioned a person during a traffic stop, but no arrests were made.\nA glove that appears to be similar to one worn by a masked man in a surveillance video outside the Guthrie home has been discovered.\nLaw enforcement also continue to investigate a series of apparent ransom notes that may have been sent from the person or people behind the alleged crime.\nThe Guthrie family has made several public pleas for her safe return, and has asked for proof that their mother is still alive.\nHere is what we know about the case.\n## When did Nancy Guthrie go missing? \nGuthrie was last seen at her home in Catalina Foothills, an affluent neighbourhood roughly six miles (10km) north-east of Tucson, on 31 January evening, when family members dropped her off around 21:30 local time (04:30 GMT).\nAt 01:47 local time on 1 February, a doorbell camera to her home was disconnected and removed.\nAt 02:28, the app on her pacemaker, an implanted cardiac device, disconnected from her phone.\nConcern grew when members of her church noticed her absence at Sunday's service and\nAfter the family called 911, authorities began a search that soon included volunteers and federal officers from Border Patrol, which stretched into the next morning. \nOfficials warned she may be in dire health without her medication. Pima County Sheriff Chris Nanos said she was \"not in good physical health\", but had no reported cognitive issues. \n## What do we know about the suspect?\nOfficials have released videos and images showing a masked and armed suspect at Nancy Guthrie's doorstep the night she disappeared.\nThe footage showed the suspect - who the FBI said appears to be a male between 5ft 9in and 5ft 10in (175-177cm) - covering a camera lens with some plants.\nHe was wearing a 25-litre Ozark Trail Hiker Pack backpack, the FBI added.\nPolice have said they found blood on Nancy Guthrie's porch and analysis confirmed it was hers.\nWatch: New video shows masked person outside Nancy Guthrie's home\nInvestigators have also discovered 16 gloves, although most were discarded by people searching for Guthrie, according to the FBI.\nHowever, DNA evidence collected from one glove, found approximately two miles from Guthrie's home, has been sent to a lab for testing, officials said. \nThe FBI says it has received preliminary DNA test results on the glove and is still waiting for \"quality control and official confirmation\" before entering an \"unknown male profile\" into its database.\n\"The one with the DNA profile recovered is different and appears to match the gloves of the subject in the surveillance video,\" the FBI said.\nOfficials have also found DNA at Guthrie's address that does not belong to her or anyone close to her.\nAs media crews have descended on the quiet Arizona suburb where the missing woman lives, authorities closed access to her street, nestled below the Santa Catalina mountains on roads lined with the state's Saguaro cactus. \nOn 12 February, authorities had put up a white tent outside the entrance to her home, though it was soon taken down. \n![Map of Arizona showing location of Catalina Foothills and above the map is a photo of Guthrie's home in the desert with annotations showing where her doorbell camera was removed and where the porch is with the blood](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/dae5/live/e497ce80-037f-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)\n## Who has been detained and released by police?\n![Reuters Santa Cruz County Sheriff's deputies block a street where a person was detained for questioning after the disappearance of Nancy Guthrie, who was later released from custody](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4233/live/3f21f9b0-072d-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)Reuters\nHours after sharing the footage of the masked person, police detained a man for questioning at a traffic stop in Arizona in connection with Guthrie's disappearance, but released him later that night, according to the BBC's US news partner, CBS News.\nAfter his release, a man, identified only as Carlos, told reporters outside his home that he did not know who Nancy Guthrie was.\n\"I hope they get the suspect, because I'm not it,\" Carlos said, according to footage posted by reporters on social media.\nA woman who identified herself as his mother-in-law said earlier that he had nothing to do with the case.\n\"They're just invading my property,\" she told CBS.\nThe Pima County Sheriff's Department had confirmed police were conducting a court-authorised search at a location in Rio Rico, south of Tucson, Arizona.\nOn 13 February, police teams searched a home approximately two miles from where Guthrie lives, however, no arrests were made. \nThe sheriff's department confirmed the next day that law enforcement officers had executed a federal court-ordered search warrant based on a lead at the residence in connection with the Guthrie case.\nThey also questioned a person during a traffic stop, but they were also released.\nThe department did not provide further details about the lead or the residents of the house, saying only that no one was arrested.\n## What did the alleged ransom notes say?\nFBI Special Agent Heith Janke said his agency had been reviewing several possible ransom notes. \nOne note, sent to several media outlets, included details about an Apple watch and a floodlight at Nancy Guthrie's home. \nPolice said one ransom note sent to the media had two deadlines: one at 17:00 on 5 February and another on\nThe FBI also said it had arrested a person for a fake ransom note on 5 February. \nOn 6 February, the FBI and the sheriff's office said they were investigating a new message and were \"inspecting the information provided in the message for its accuracy\".\nThey did not say who received the note or provide any details about what it said, but a presenter for a local affiliate of CBS News told the BBC that her channel received the new \"message\". The presenter did not disclose what was in the message. \nIn several emotional videos, Savannah Guthrie and her siblings have commented on the alleged ransom notes and pleaded for their mother's safe return. \n\"We are ready to talk,\" Guthrie said in a 4 February video, addressing the abductor or abductors directly. She added, \"we need to know without a doubt that she is alive and you have her... Please, reach out to us.\"\nIn \nOn 6 February,[ the family released a third video](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/ce9y99eg0nno), with Savannah Guthrie saying: \"We received your message, and we understand. We beg you now to return our mother to us so that we can celebrate with her.\" \nShe added: \"And we will pay.\"\nSheriff: Savannah Guthrie\u2019s mother 'didn\u2019t go willingly'\nOn 9 February, Savannah Guthrie released another video pleading for the public to help . \n\"I just want to share a few thoughts as we enter another week of this nightmare,\" she said in a the video posted on Instagram. \"She was taken, and we don't know where. And we need your help.\"\nOn 12 February, she posted another video featuring footage of her mom as a young woman with Savannah and her sister as children. The video also shows a photo of the three Guthrie siblings as children with their mother. \n\"Our lovely mom,\" Guthrie wrote alongside the video. \"We will never give up on her. Thank you for your prayers and hope.\"\nThe FBI is offering a reward of $100,000 (\u00a372,000) for information that leads to an arrest. The FBI says it has received more than 13,000 tips from the public since her disappearance.\nPresident Donald Trump, who ordered federal authorities help in the investigation, has called the case \"very unusual\".\n## Who is Savannah Guthrie?\nGuthrie, 54, is an American broadcast journalist who has served as the co-anchor of NBC News' Today since 2012. She is also the network's chief legal correspondent and a primary anchor for its election coverage. Previously, she was NBC News' White House correspondent. \nGuthrie was born in Australia, but when she was a toddler her family moved to Tucson, Arizona, where she grew up for most of her childhood. She did not travel back to Australia until 2015, visiting with her mum Nancy and fulfilling her \"lifelong dream to return\", she told the Daily Telegraph. \n\"For my mum to come back and get to see where she used to live brings back a lot of happy memories with my dad,\" Guthrie told the Daily Telegraph. \nGuthrie, who maintains a public stance of political neutrality, has interviewed presidents Barack Obama, Joe Biden and Trump. \nShe has a law degree from Georgetown University and a bachelor's degree in journalism from the University of Arizona. \nShe has won an Edward R Murrow Award for ethical electronic journalism and multiple News Emmys, including for an interview with Trump a month before the 2020 election.\n## [Alleged ransom notes investigated in disappearance of Savannah {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cj3vve6v17xo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# 'Dragged out and set on fire' - the Bangladesh mob killing that shocked the world\n![Anahita Sachdev/BBC Dipu Chandra Das with his wife, Meghna and daughter Gitika pose for a family picture](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/95b9/live/b5be2ca0-0337-11f1-9d05-c7b737bbaef1.jpg.webp)Anahita Sachdev/BBC\nDipu Chandra Das poses for a family picture with wife Meghna and daughter Gitika\nThe morning before he died, Dipu Chandra Das left home at first light, stepping out of his tin-sheet house in Bangladesh's Mymensingh city, overlooking a warren of lanes off the highway from Dhaka.\nThe 28-year-old woke up his father, said goodbye to his wife, cradled his 18-month-old daughter. Then he boarded a bus for the 60km (37-mile) journey to the garment factory where he worked as a junior quality inspector, checking sweaters bound for global high-street brands such as H&M and Next.\nHis family would not see him again.\n_**Warning: Some readers may find the details below disturbing**_\nTwenty-four hours later, on 18 December, Das, a Hindu, was dead - lynched and burned by a mob after being accused of blasphemy.\nAccused of insulting the Prophet Muhammad, he was dragged from his workplace, beaten, hauled more than a kilometre through crowded streets, tied to a tree on a busy highway and set alight before hundreds of onlookers.\nThe killing sparked global outrage, particularly across the border in India, reviving fears about the safety of minorities since then prime minister Sheikh Hasina was toppled in student-led protests last year. About 9% of Bangladesh's 174 million people belong to religious minorities - mostly Hindus. Relations with the Muslim majority have long been marked by periodic tension and insecurity.\n![Shahidul Alam/Drik/Majority World Meghna Das wife of Dipu Chandra Das, who was brutally murdered by a mob after being accused of insulting religion\nPhoto: Shahidul Alam/Drik/Majority World\n](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/be1a/live/d0b5aad0-0272-11f1-8cb0-d70684951333.jpg.webp)Shahidul Alam/Drik/Majority World\nMeghna Das says Dipu told her that he was 'happy at his work' in the factory\nFifty days on, the outrage has ebbed, but grief hangs over the home Das left behind - a single dark room with a beaten-earth floor and tin roof, where the family has lived for nearly 15 years.\nIt is a house with little furniture or belongings: a plastic table and chairs, beds, sacks of rice, a teddy bear, clothes hanging from a single rail. A refrigerator and a small television - both bought by Dipu on instalments - stand out, quiet markers of a future he was still trying to build.\nHis mother, Shefali Rani Das, breaks down as soon as visitors enter.\n\"Oh Dipu, where is my Dipu?\" she cries, collapsing into a mournful lament.\nDipu was the eldest son of Rabi Das, a 54-year-old labourer who has spent his life hauling sacks of rice, wheat and vegetables at a nearby market for 400 to 500 taka ($3- $4) a day.\nYears of hard work have left him weather-beaten and broken. Dipu wanted him to stop.\n\"Now I am working,\" he would tell his father often. \"You rest.\"\nDipu would hand over his salary to the family. He talked constantly about building a proper house, one that would lift the family out of mud and tin for good.\nBorn at home and raised in a mixed Hindu-Muslim neighbourhood shaped by quiet hardship, Dipu was, by all accounts, a private man with few friends. He left college during the pandemic as lockdowns crushed the family's finances. \nBy 2024, he was working at a sweater factory, sending money home, and returning from his dorm with chocolates for his infant daughter, spending evenings watching cartoons on TV.\nThe eldest of three sons, his ambition, his mother said, was to see his younger brothers, Apu, 22, and Rithick, 16, \"settled\". \n![Anahita Sachdev/BBC Shefali Rani Das with a picture of her son, Dipu, in a suit after he got his job ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/dc79/live/9f288640-074f-11f1-8fcf-193eb7dfcf8f.jpg.webp)Anahita Sachdev/BBC\nShefali Rani Das with a picture of her son, Dipu, in a new suit after he got his job \nDipu was a small cog in Bangladesh's garment export machine, working for the past 14 months at the Pioneer Knitwear factory. The unit employs about 8,500 workers and is one of nine factories in a group with a workforce of 47,000.\nIts sweaters, stitched on long production lines, retail in shops across the US and Europe. Dipu, earning 13,500 taka ($110; \u00a380) a month, checked stitches and seams on one of the factory's hundred production lines. He was one of 868 Hindu workers there.\nIt was an ordinary life, lived carefully - a young man trying to pull his family out of poverty.\nThen came the fateful December evening. A rumour swept through his workplace and outside that he had uttered a 'katukti' - Bengali for an insulting remark - against Prophet Muhammad. \nWhat happened next, in the hours between the allegation and his death, is now the subject of a police investigation.\nOn the evening in question, a casual chat about weekend plans among three female co-workers near closing time took an uncomfortable turn when Dipu joined in and allegedly made a remark later deemed offensive to the Prophet, according to Mohammed Abdullah Al-Mamun, the additional superintendent of police in Mymensingh, citing accounts from at least three witnesses.\n![ANAHITA SACHDEV/BBC The mob took out Dipu from the side gate \\(right\\) of the garment factory where he worked](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8354/live/a476dca0-074f-11f1-8fcf-193eb7dfcf8f.jpg.webp)ANAHITA SACHDEV/BBC\nThe mob took out Dipu from the side gate on the right of the garment factory where he worked\nFactory CCTV footage shows Dipu clocking out about 30 minutes after this conversation. He later returned to the floor - footage from two hours later shows him roaming the area, according to Uday Hossain, a senior factory manager.\nWhy Dipu went back after checking out remains unclear.\nOutside a mob had begun to collect as word spread that a factory worker had committed blasphemy. (Bangladesh has no formal blasphemy law, but it criminalises acts \"intended to outrage religious feelings\".) \nAs workers poured out at the end of the day, the rumour travelled fast in a busy neighbourhood. By about 6pm, tension had hardened both inside the factory and on the street.\n\"What followed went far beyond the law,\" said Al-Mamun.\nA mob that began as several hundred at the factory gates demanding Dipu be handed over quickly swelled to more than a thousand, drawing in onlookers from nearby areas. CCTV footage shows men trying to force the gates and hurling ropes over them to climb inside.\nAt around 20.42 local time, Hossain says, the crowd used shovels to prise open a smaller side gate, poured into the factory, and \"carried Dipu away like a wave\". \nHossain said the factory had alerted police at least 45 minutes earlier. Yet even as industrial police and officers in plain clothes arrived at the site, they were unable to extract him from the mob, he added.\n![Shahidul Alam/Drik/Majority World This is the tree, on the main road at Square Masterbari, Bhaluka, Mymensingh, from which the corpse of Dipu Chandra Das was hung and then set on fire. \nPhoto: Shahidul Alam/Drik/Majority World\n](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/4b47/live/898e07b0-0272-11f1-8cb0-d70684951333.jpg.webp)Shahidul Alam/Drik/Majority World\nThe tree on a busy highway where Dipu's body was hung and set on fire\nPolice, however, offer a slightly different account of how Dipu ended up in the mob's hands.\nThey say the crowd threatened to break down the gates if he was not handed over. Faced with that ultimatum, factory workers opened the gate and released him, according to Al-Mamun.\nInvestigators believe Dipu was beaten to death outside the factory before his body was dragged to a nearby highway, tied with a rope to a tree, and set on fire. \"By the time I arrived, he was already dead,\" Al-Mamun said.\nSo far, 22 people have been arrested in connection with the incident. Half of those held were Dipu's co-workers at the factory, including two managers of the floor where he worked. An imam of a local mosque has also been arrested.\nMost of the suspects are aged between 22 and 30. Police estimate that around 150 people were directly involved in the attack, with many more present as onlookers, while others are still being sought by police.\nFew of those arrested, Al-Mamun said, appeared \"particularly religious\".\n\"Some are students, some are passersby, some are locals. Everyone was beating Dipu, so they beat him too. But we are treating this as a hate crime.\"\n![ANAHITA SACHDEV/BBC Rithick, Dipu's youngest brother, cradles 18-month-old Meghna outside their home in Mymensingh](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/27f1/live/8ae898c0-07cf-11f1-85fe-fb2991e2203e.jpg.webp)ANAHITA SACHDEV/BBC\nRithick, Dipu's youngest brother, cradles 18-month-old Meghna outside their home in Mymensingh\nSince the student uprising of 2024, the scale and nature of attacks on minorities - mainly Hindus - have become a fiercely contested question in Bangladesh.\nThe outgoing interim government says that between January and December 2025, police records show 645 incidents involving minorities but insists that nearly nine in 10 were not communal.\nOfficials say that most cases involved ordinary criminality - land disputes, theft, extortion, or personal feuds - that were later framed as religious violence. By its count, only 71 incidents had a clear communal element, including 38 cases of temple vandalism, eight attacks on temples, and one killing.\nHuman-rights groups paint a darker picture.\nAin o Salish Kendra (ASK) records 42 incidents of violence against Hindus in 2025, including dozens of attacks on homes and arson, leaving one dead and 15 injured - numbers that broadly overlap with, but are narrower than, the government's tally.\nThe biggest divergence comes from the Bangladesh Hindu Buddhist Christian Unity Council, which says violence spiked dramatically after August 2024.\nMainly drawing on media reports, the group says it has documented 2,711 attacks on minorities since August 2024 - including at least 92 killings, 133 attacks on temples and 47 cases of land grabbing - figures far higher than official estimates.\n\"Minorities in Bangladesh have faced attacks by religious extremists for more than half a century. Governments of all stripes share responsibility. Our numbers have steadily shrunk as many have fled or migrated,\" Manindra Kumar Nath of the Council told the BBC.\nIndia, meanwhile, says independent sources have documented more than 2,900 incidents of violence against minorities - including killings, arson and land grabs - during the interim government's tenure.\n![NurPhoto via Getty Images Students of Dhaka University take part in a protest march in the Dhaka University area of Dhaka, Bangladesh, on December 21, 2025, demanding justice over alleged mob violence, including the killing of Dipu Chandra Das following false blasphemy accusations and the death of seven-year-old Ayesha in an arson attack on a BNP worker's house. \\(Photo by MD Abu Sufian Jewel/NurPhoto via Getty Images\\)](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ef59/live/cc7f02d0-0750-11f1-8fcf-193eb7dfcf8f.jpg.webp)NurPhoto via Getty Images\nThere were protests against the killing of Dipu Das in Dhaka in December \nNobel laureate and outgoing interim government chief Muhammad Yunus has said, \"there's no anti-Hindu violence\", dismissing such reports as \"fake news\" by the Indian media. Separately, he has said the attacks were \nYet, all is not lost. Dipu's killing sparked protests in Dhaka; his employers have cleared his dues and promised to build the house he dreamed of. The outgoing government has\n\"What happened was barbaric, deplorable and shameful. We want the strictest punishment for those responsible,\" said Badshah Mian, managing director of Pioneer Knitwear. \"If this can happen outside a factory, none of us is safe.\"\nThat note of solidarity has persisted despite wider tensions. \nAfter Hasina fled the country in 2024, Hindu minorities - often seen as aligned with her secular Awami League in an Islamic state - were attacked by rivals, even as some young Muslim groups moved to protect Hindu homes and shrines.\nAnd ahead of the recently concluded election, Tarique Rahman of the Bangladesh Nationalist Party (BNP) pledged: \"We want to build a Bangladesh together - the kind of Bangladesh a mother dreams of,\" reaching out to people of all religions.\n![Reuters Hindu activists hold placards and Indian national flags, as they chant the slogan, \"Stop atrocities on Hindus\", and protest attacks on religious places of Hindus in Bangladesh, in Ahmedabad, India August 13, 2024. REUTERS/Amit Dave](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/959a/live/9dea2960-f826-11f0-b5f7-49f0357294ff.jpg.webp)Reuters\nDipu Das's lynching sparked global outrage, including protests across the border in India\nBack at Dipu's home, the night of the killing is recalled in fragments.\nA phone call around eight in the evening. A visit to the police station. A father stumbling home to deliver news that shattered the household.\nHis parents had collapsed. For hours, they were unconscious, neighbours later said - revived with water, then saline injections as the house filled with people and cries.\nNearly two months on, Dipu's mother still breaks down every day. His father has not returned to work. Sleep has vanished. So has appetite, routine, peace.\n\"Our life has stalled. Nothing is moving anymore,\" says Rabi Das.\n_Follow BBC India on_ _,__,__and_\n[Hinduism](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c2rndjjldzyt)\n[Asia](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c5rznn0nvvyt)\n[Bangladesh](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c77jz3md7grt)\n[India](https://www.bbc.com/news/world/asia/india)\n[Islam](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cxw7vreqxpnt)\n[Blasphemy](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/czlg3rqkxlet)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Hindu temple left 'heartbroken' over site's sale](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c3wlq2x2zjzo)\n## [Council agrees to sell Hindu temple site again](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cn0zdejj7j7o)\n## [Temple sale decision 'made without all information'](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c74wqxw4d08o)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 2 hrs ago ![Festive lighting hung in central London with the wording Happy Ramadan.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/67c7/live/b8a5c6c0-0a8b-11f1-8b85-1da9bd0ede97.jpg) Ramadan events across the North West The Islamic holy month of Ramadan starts with the new moon, when Muslims fast from dawn to sunset. ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c5yqdyed5zko)\n[ 2 hrs ago ![A man dressed in a suit with a navy head covering stands at a lectern to deliver a speech. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/clyzen2lgw9o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Female Israeli soldiers rescued after being chased by ultra-Orthodox men\nProtesters, police and military clash\nTwo female Israeli soldiers had to be rescued by police after being chased by a crowd of ultra-Orthodox Jewish men.\nFootage from the city of Bnei Brak showed the women running through streets strewn with rubbish and overturned bins as police officers formed a protective barrier. More than 20 people were arrested.\nReports suggest the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) soldiers were wrongly believed to be trying to deliver army conscription orders. Military service is mandatory for most Jewish Israelis, but ultra\u2011Orthodox Jews have long been exempt. Moves to reform this have caused outrage among the community.\nIsraeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu condemned the incident as \"unacceptable\".\n![Reuters Three ultra-Orthodox Jewish children run from a stun grenade explosion, one covers his ears. The grenade has exploded into a small ball of light a short distance from the young boys.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d42a/live/7139f310-0aa9-11f1-adb1-dfcb996ae067.jpg.webp)Reuters\nPolice used stun grenades to disperse the crowds in the city of Bnei Brak\n\"This is an extreme minority that does not represent the entire Haredi [ultra-Orthodox] community,\" Netanyahu said in a post on X. \n\"We will not allow anarchy, and we will not tolerate any harm to IDF servicemen and security forces who carry out their duties with dedication and determination.\"\nJewish religious leaders also condemned the actions of those involved in the unrest.\nRiot police in Bnei Brak, on the edge of Tel Aviv, used stun grenades against the crowd and arrested 23 people, police said in a statement.\nThree officers were injured and several police vehicles damaged, including a patrol car that was overturned and a police motorcycle that was set on fire, police added.\nThe IDF servicewomen had been on an official home visit to another soldier when the confrontation broke out, Israeli broadcaster Kan reported.\n![Reuters Dozens of ultra-Orthodox Jews form a crowd in the streets, a stun grenade disperses in front of them, creating a plume of smoke and sparks to fly.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/2a78/live/5c60c480-0ab5-11f1-8a5a-ef57761b4594.jpg)Reuters\nThe ultra-Orthodox population now accounts for 14% of the Israeli population\nLate last year, \nThe issue of conscription has become increasingly contentious during the war in Gaza, with the Israeli government now debating draft legislation that would require ultra-Orthodox men not in full-time religious study to serve.\nSince the State of Israel was declared in 1948, students enrolled full-time at a religious school, or yeshiva, have been exempted from conscription. \nThat exemption was ruled unconstitutional by Israel's High Court of Justice more than a decade ago. Temporary arrangements to continue it were formally ended by the court last year, forcing the government to begin conscripting the community.\nThe ultra-Orthodox population has more than doubled its share of Israel's population over the past seven decades, and now accounts for 14%.\n[Israel](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c302m85q5ljt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Iran must abandon enriched uranium and not produce more, Netanyahu says](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cly191kz1lpo)\n## [Eleven killed in Israeli strikes on Gaza, rescuers say](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c8r15eyvk2do)\n## [Hundreds of thousands join Iran protests around the world](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/czr0lykl4g4o)\n* * *\n", "metadata": {"title": "Female Israeli soldiers rescued after being chased by ultra-Orthodox men", "description": "The two women were helped by police after being forced to run from a mob in the city of Bnei Brak.", "keywords": null, "author": null, "og:title": "Female Israeli soldiers rescued after being chased by ultra-Orthodox men", "og:description": "The two women were helped by police after being forced to run from a mob in the city of Bnei Brak.", "og:image": "https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/1024/branded_news/d42a/live/7139f310-0aa9-11f1-adb1-dfcb996ae067.jpg", "og:image:width": "1024", "og:image:height": "576", "og:image:alt": "Three ultra-Orthodox Jewish children run from a stun grenade explosion, one covers his ears. but reports of attacks in other parts of the country have risen sharply more recently.\nBandits swooped on the village of Tunga-Makeri early in the morning, before striking the nearby villages of Konkoso and Pissa, local officials said.\nPolice said six people were killed in one incident, and 20 more in the attacks on Konkoso and Pisa.\nOfficials confirmed at least 29 people had been killed as of Saturday, but Saidu said that death toll could rise.\nThe number of people abducted is also unknown because many residents fled their homes and ran into the nearby bush or neighbouring communities, he said.\n\"People are afraid because you can't tell which community is going to be next,\" he added. \nA security report cited by AFP news agency said bandits came on 41 motorcycles, each carrying two or three men.\nAbdullahi Rofia, a resident of neighbouring Agwara, told the BBC that many displaced people have taken shelter in his community, which was itself attacked two weeks ago.\n\"People are so traumatised, they no longer go to farm nor do they go to market,\" he said.\n\"The bandits are not interesting in stealing or looting - they are more interested in killing and terrorising locals.\"\nAuthorities have introduced emergency measures, including a restriction on late-night gatherings and a \"partial curfew\" that bans motorcycle taxis from operating after 20:00 local time (19:00 GMT).\nPolice confirmed that security teams have been deployed and rescue efforts are ongoing.\nNigeria's leaders are under pressure to curb violence, with jihadist groups active in the north-west and separatist insurgents based in the country's south-east.\nThe US launched Christmas Day strikes targeting Islamist militants in Nigeria's northern Sokoto state and President Donald Trump warned of further attacks \"if they continue to kill Christians\". \nMany of the victims of jihadist violence are Muslim, according to organisations monitoring political violence in Nigeria.\nA Nigerian official \nIt came after more than 250 children and staff were abducted from a Catholic school in Papiri, in one of the largest recent mass-kidnappings. "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Cuban cigar festival called off as US blockade worsens energy crisis\n![Getty Images A woman distrubutes cigars at the closing ceremony of the 2025 Habanos Festival](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/a2c6/live/718b9130-09c7-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThe event was due to take place over five days in late February\nAn annual cigar festival in Cuban capital Havana has become the latest casualty of a fuel shortage caused by a US oil blockade.\nThe organising committee of the Festival del Habano announced on Saturday that this year's event - which was due to take place over five days in late February - would be postponed until further notice. \nIt said the decision was motivated \"by the complex economic situation\" facing Cuba due to the \"economic, commercial and financial blockade\" by the US.\nA fuel shortage causing power cuts on the Caribbean island has been worsened by the US seizing oil shipments from Cuba's long-standing ally Venezuela.\nCuba also has a shortage of aviation fuel, leading several airlines to suspend services there, while some countries, including the UK, have warned against non-essential travel to the island.\nCuban cigars are regarded as among the finest in the world, but are illegal in the US due to long-standing American trade embargoes. \nMore than 1,300 people from around 70 countries are estimated to attend the Habano Festival each year to sample cigars from Cuba's producers, as well as visit tobacco plantations and factories.\nWith international tourism to the island nation limited by the US embargo, the festival's organisers said they hoped to wait until conditions had improved.\nThe organising committee said it was working on setting a new date, which has yet to be announced.\n\"The priority of the Habano Festival is to offer its participants a comprehensive experience at the height of the relevance and prestige that this event represents internationally,\" it said.\n\"The postponement of this celebration is a measure aimed at protecting this experience.\"\nVenezuela was previously believed to have sent around 35,000 barrels of oil a day to Cuba before the US military seized former president Nicol\u00e1s Maduro on 3 January.\nPower cuts lasting up to 18 hours a day have since affected hospital emergency wards, dialysis patients and pumping stations.\nUS President Donald Trump has urged Cuban leaders to \"make a deal\" or face unspecified consequences.\nUN human rights experts have described Washington's restrictions on Cuba's oil imports as an \"extreme form of unilateral economic coercion\".\nThe US and Cuba have had a strained relationship since the communist Fidel Castro overthrew a US-backed government in 1959. US economic and trade embargoes on Cuba have been in place since 1960.\nWhile steps were taken to improve diplomatic relations, particularly under former US President Barack Obama, the Trump administration has reversed many of these moves.\n[Cuba](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c40rjmqdqnlt)\n[Oil](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cmjpj223708t)\n[United He faces 59 charges relating to the Bondi Beach shooting, including 15 counts of murder and one of committing a terrorist attack.\nNaveed and his father, Sajid, [allegedly attacked a Hanukkah event at the famous beach on 14 December](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c3r711lnr8zo), killing 15 people and injuring more than 40.\nSajid, 50, was shot dead by police at the scene, while Naveed was critically injured and later transferred from hospital to prison.\nMonday was the first time Akram had been seen on screen and spoken since his arrest \u2013 saying just one word when Deputy Chief Magistrate Sharon Freund asked if he heard a discussion about an extension of suppression orders: \"Yeah\".\nThe court orders cover the identities of those who survived the shooting, but allow the survivors to self-identify if they wish.\nOutside court, Akram's lawyer, Ben Archbold, said it was too early to say what plea his client would enter, according to the Australian Broadcasting Corporation.\nHe said Akram was doing \"as well as can be expected\" while in custody in Goulburn supermax prison.\n\"Everyone knows it's supermax \u2026 very onerous conditions,\" Archbold told journalists.\nWhen asked if Akram had given a police interview, he said \"All we've done is start the process. We're waiting for the brief to be served. There's nothing more I can say.\"\n## ['It was pandemonium': Jewish community in shock after deadly Bondi Beach attack ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c3r711lnr8zo)\n## [Bondi bravery: Lifeguards, October - was described as showing the men sitting in front of an image of an Islamic State group (IS) flag.\nThey could be heard making statements about their motivations for the attack and condemning \"the acts of 'Zionists'\", police said.\nPolice said separate footage from October showed the father and son \"conducting firearms training in a countryside location\", believed to be in New South Wales.\nThey were seen \"firing shotguns and moving in a tactical manner\", officials added.\nAuthorities earlier confirmed that two rabbis, a Holocaust survivor and a 10-year-old girl were among the victims of the Bondi Beach attack.\nAkram is next due in court in April.\n[Australia](https://www.bbc.com/news/world/australia)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Sydney police plead for return of man, Many have been lauded for their realism.\nBut the trend has also [sparked alarm from several Hollywood studios](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cjd9nllng22o) that have accused the AI platform's makers of copyright infringement.\nOn Friday, Disney sent a cease-and-desist letter to ByteDance accusing it of supplying Seedance with a \"pirated library\" of the studio's copyrighted characters, including those from Marvel and Star Wars.\nDisney's lawyers accused ByteDance of committing a \"virtual smash-and-grab\" of their intellectual property, including superheroes from Marvel, Star Wars and various cartoons.\nOn Monday ByteDance told the BBC that the company \"respects intellectual property rights and we have heard the concerns regarding Seedance 2.0.\"\n\"We are taking steps to strengthen current safeguards as we work to prevent the unauthorised use of intellectual property and likeness by users.\"\nByteDance did not respond to questions asking for details on the safeguards it plans to implement.\n![Reddit/AI-generated image An AI-generated image of Star Wars' Anakin holding a red lightsabre to the back of Rey, who is on her knees during a fight on a rooftop overlooking shows a fight between Anakin Skywalker and Rey from Star Wars\nLike other generative-AI tools, Seedance can create videos based on short text prompts.\nMany of Seedance's clips are based on real actors and shows and some have gone viral since the launch of its latest 2.0 version on 12 February. \nThe BBC has found clips online said to have been generated by Seedance showing Star Wars characters Anakin Skywalker and Rey battling with their lightsabres, and Spider-Man fighting Captain America on the streets of New York.\nThe company has not disclosed what data it uses to train Seedance.\nByteDance had previously said the product had already paused the ability for users to upload images of real people. \nThe company also said it respects intellectual property rights and copyright protections, and takes any potential infringement seriously.\n## [Hollywood writers fear losing work to AI](https://www.bbc.com/news/business-66289583)\n## [DeepSeek: The Chinese AI app that has the world talking](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c5yv5976z9po)\nDisney's legal threat follows criticism from other organisations in Hollywood over the Seedance platform.\nThe Motion Picture Association, which represents major US studios like Warner Bros Discovery, Paramount and Netflix, has demanded that the tool \"immediately cease its infringing activity\".\nActors' union Sag-Aftra has also accused Seedance of \"blatant infringement\".\nMeanwhile the Japanese government has launched an investigation into the Chinese firm over potential copyright violations, after AI-generated videos of popular Japanese anime characters showed up on online.\nOther AI image\u2011generation tools have likewise been hit with legal action.\nLast year, Disney and NBCUniversal [sued AI image generator Midjourney](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cg5vjqdm1ypo), accusing the platform of generating \"endless unauthorised copies\" of the studios' copyrighted works. The case is ongoing.\nDisney has also asked Google to restrict the generation of its characters on Google's AI platforms.\n[International Business](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c4nrdv135zet)\n[Copyright](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c4wl5zqxv9xt)\n[China](https://www.bbc.com/news/world/asia/china)\n[Artificial intelligence](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/ce1qrvleleqt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Bedding firm backs down on 'Swift Home' trademark after Taylor Swift appeal](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/crm87109l3wo)\n## [Trump has big plans for Venezuela's oil but are they feasible?](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c4gjx1j1nkjo)\n## [Inflation eases in US as prices for used cars fall](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cd6z05p56xyo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 20 hrs ago ![Two men walk past departures board at Beijing airport](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/f3cb/live/db4e5850-0a67-11f1-ac70-650e103cdf93.jpg) China confirms visa-free travel for UK and Canadian nationals Relaxed visa rules for British and Canadian passport holders will come into force on 17 February.  ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c875d3d3x34o)\n[ 1 day ago ![Women wearing traditional costumes pose for photographs in front of a Chinese temple](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8573/live/9c451730-0985-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg) Is this a 'very Chinese time in your life'? {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cn8z7exgg9vo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Red eyes and black beaches: How one of the worst environmental disasters left its mark\n![Huw Evans Agency A striking photo from the air of the Sea Empress tanker, surrounded by tugboats and vast swathes of oil polluting the sea around it.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/f32a/live/79361d10-fe00-11f0-bb32-2b2b28af4780.jpg.webp)Huw Evans Agency\nMore than 70,000 tonnes of crude oil spilled out of the Sea Empress tanker after it ran aground near Milford Haven in Pembrokeshire in February 1996\nWhen the oil tanker Sea Empress ran aground 30 years ago, some of south Wales' best-known beaches turned black.\nThousands of seabirds were killed off a globally significant stretch of coastline for wildlife in Pembrokeshire, while salvage crews fought for almost a week to lift the stricken tanker off the rocks and tow her to safety.\nDescribed as \"a wake up call\" that led to changes in public attitude towards the environment, to this day there is anger and debate over how the disaster could have happened.\nExperts warn climate change increases the risk of major pollution events in future on busier and more hazardous seas.\n![Getty Images Oil coating a beach and rocks after the Sea Empress disaster in February 1996](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/148e/live/8f8092b0-09d8-11f1-bfd9-a1813d3ca61e.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThe oil slick affected an estimated 200km of the Welsh coastline\n\"It's all coming back to me 30 years later,\" said retired BBC reporter Hefin Wyn, looking out to sea at St Anne's Head, a remote spot at the entrance to Milford Haven waterway, one of the busiest ports in the UK.\nHe recalled \"the humming of the tanker\" against the lashing wind and rain and then the \"heart-breaking\" sight of \"thick, treacle-like oil\" on the sea.\n\"One could see the birds landing [on the water]\u2026 but they couldn't fly away,\" he said.\n\"It was a wake-up call - by now we are far more aware of the need to look after the environment than we were at the time.\" \n![Retired BBC Wales reporter Hefin Wyn at St Anne's Head, Pembrokeshire.  He has white hair and a white beard and is wearing a green, waterproof coat.  Behind him can be seen the entrance to the Milford Haven waterway.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/2e75/live/b6dc8430-05c4-11f1-b8f3-e5c6454cb301.jpg.webp)\nHefin Wyn, from Maenclochog in Pembrokeshire, reported on the disaster for the BBC's Welsh language news programmes\nIt was \"the single worst incident I've ever seen\", said conservationist and TV presenter Iolo Williams, who worked at the time for the RSPB in Wales.\nThe oil \"got into [the pores of] your skin, it got into your hair... and everybody went around with these bright red eyes\", he told [a new BBC documentary marking the 30th anniversary](https://www.bbc.com/mediacentre/proginfo/2026/07/black-waters-sea-empress-disaster).\nAbout 7,000 oil-coated birds were picked up along the coast but \"the amount killed would have been at least double that because a lot of them would have sunk offshore,\" he said.\n![Getty Images Rescue of an oiled duck following the sinking of the Sea Empress](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/e618/live/664fb710-09ef-11f1-b95c-79d90fbdbf52.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nAbout 7,000 birds were collected covered in oil but less than half of them survived \nIn rockpools, starfish, limpets and other wildlife were smothered - while Pembrokeshire's fishing industry was brought to a standstill for more than 18 months.\nThe disaster happened in what is the UK's only coastal national park, with 35 Sites of special scientific interest (SSSIs), a marine nature reserve and islands home to internationally significant seabird populations. \n![Mirrorpix Swans coated in oil stand on newspaper in the back of a van ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ff4d/live/c5881cf0-084a-11f1-ba49-bb79b430dd38.jpg.webp)Mirrorpix\nThe RSPCA ran a makeshift hospital from an old industrial unit in Milford Haven where birds were taken to be nursed and cleaned\n## What happened when the Sea Empress ran aground?\nThe 274m (900ft) long, Liberian-registered tanker Sea Empress was on its way to an oil refinery when it hit rocks at the entrance to Milford Haven waterway at 20:07 on 15 February 1996.\nThe initial response focused on trying to bring another vessel alongside to offload the Sea Empress' cargo of crude oil.\nBut gale force winds led to the crew and tugboats having to evacuate and the ship was left to ride out the storm for several hours.\nEyewitnesses described the tanker \"swinging around like a compass\" as waves crashed over the abandoned ship and black plumes of oil spurted out into the sea.\n![Black Waters: The Sea Empress Disaster A TV showing a news piece from 1996 illustrating where the Sea Empress had run aground.  ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8e09/live/c369a320-0670-11f1-a360-89dc08e51674.jpg.webp)Black Waters: The Sea Empress Disaster\nAs shown in news footage from the time, the Sea Empress ran aground near Milford Haven and close to Skomer and Skokholm islands, havens for rare seabirds\nThere were repeated attempts in the days that followed to refloat the vessel.\nIt was finally towed to a jetty on 21 February, ending six days of dramatic scenes which had made headlines around the world.\nMore than 70,000 tonnes - or 32 Olympic-sized swimming pools worth - of oil had spilled into the sea, affecting an estimated 200km (124 miles) of coastline.\n![Getty Images The Liberian-registered, Russian-crewed oil tanker, run aground 15 February near an offshore wildlife refuge off the Welsh coast](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d864/live/e74241c0-05b3-11f1-8336-6d2201ebafb2.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nIt took six days for rescue teams to move the 147,000-ton Sea Empress off the rocks\n\"It was such a shock to the local community,\" said Gordon James, then director of Friends of the Earth in Wales.\n\"I remember going down to Tenby and seeing the beautiful North Beach covered in black and there were people there in tears.\n\"We set up a legal team and we interviewed a lot of people... and the evidence was damning.\n\"The tugboat men and the marine pilots, they'd issued warnings that safety was being compromised [at the port], there were cutbacks and they were saying more or less it's a disaster waiting to happen.\"\nAn inexperienced pilot had been tasked with taking the tanker into Milford Haven and \"then we learnt that the radar [at the port] hadn't been working properly for months\".\nMilford Haven Port Authority was eventually fined \u00a34m, which at the time was the biggest penalty ever issued for a pollution case in Britain.\nIt was later reduced on appeal to \u00a3750,000 to enable the port to make changes to improve safety.\n![Getty Images Cleanup operation along the Pembrokeshire coastline after 72,000 tonnes of crude oil was released into the sea ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/6dea/live/dfcf8f50-05b4-11f1-8336-6d2201ebafb2.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nAbout 1,000 people spent weeks working to remove oil from the beaches \nJames still feels angry at what he sees as mistakes made during the response to the incident.\nHe insists the tanker should not have been held in place before the weather turned.\nLocal pilots and tugboat crews had said they could take the vessel further out to sea or to a jetty to offload the oil but \"were ignored\", he claimed.\n\"The storm came in and they lost control.\"\n![Gordon James, ex director of Friends of the Earth Cymru sat at his desk looking through documents and pictures from the time of the Sea Empress disaster](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/ce26/live/bb86c5d0-05d9-11f1-a351-bb43301b14c5.jpg.webp)\n\"Looking back over 30 years, I still feel anger that it happened,\" says Gordon James, then director of Friends of the Earth in Wales\n## 'Risk of explosion'\nJoe Small sees things differently.\nHe was senior operations manager for the the UK's Marine Pollution Control Unit and one of the first people to step on board the Sea Empress after it ran aground.\nIt became apparent upon inspecting the ship that oil had moved internally into tanks not designed to hold it, presenting \"a huge safety issue\" and \"risk of explosion\".\nThat limited their options, he said, adding that they wanted to make sure the ship was safe before bringing it into a populated area such as Milford Haven.\nThe salvage operation was \"a huge technical challenge\" and the fact it was managed within a week was \"pretty good going\", he claimed.\n![Getty Images The Sea Empress pictured listing to side, with tug boats on either side](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/0dd8/live/67b698d0-05b7-11f1-8336-6d2201ebafb2.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThe disaster still ranks in the top 20 worst oil spills worldwide\nTom Sawyer, the Port of Milford Haven's chief executive, called the Sea Empress disaster \"a defining moment\", saying it had \"exposed shortcomings in our industry and caused profound harm to a coastline whose natural beauty and biodiversity we all treasure\".\nThe port had \nThe disaster led to major changes in how the UK prepares for and responds to maritime incidents, while the shipping industry worldwide \"accelerated the adoption of double-hull tankers, mandatory emergency towing points and improved compensation frameworks for environmental damage,\" Sawyer said.\n![Mirrorpix A man and two children watch as the Sea Empress is towed to a jetty ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/e892/live/fc426940-09d9-11f1-bfd9-a1813d3ca61e.jpg.webp)Mirrorpix\nThe Sea Empress disaster changed how the UK prepares for and responds to maritime incidents\n## Could a major oil spill happen again?\n\"We're constantly looking for ways to improve, better prepare and make things safer,\" said Joe Small.\nFiercer and more frequent storms brought by climate change was a major challenge - as are busier and more hazardous seas, he warned.\nThe loss of oil refining capacity in the UK meant a greater need to import refined products like petrol, which comes in on smaller tankers.\n\"We're now having four or five vessels to carry the same volume [as the Sea Empress],\" he said.\n\"More ships, worse weather - no matter how safe our operations are, things go wrong\".\nGlobally tankers were facing \"a resurgence in piracy and other attacks\", while a newer phenomenon of so-called \"\n## 'The real price of oil'\nThe Sea Empress disaster could have been much worse.\nThe time of year it happened meant many key migratory birds had not arrived back to Pembrokeshire's cliffs and islands - and the wind direction blew some of the oil away from the coast.\nA massive effort by the community to clean up the beaches was seen as a huge success, allowing the county to recover far more quickly than had been feared.\nFirst Minister Eluned Morgan, then a member of the European Parliament for mid and west Wales, revealed she still had a pair of wellington boots \"with Sea Empress oil on them\" after helping out.\nIt had shown \"how important it is that we treat our seas with respect\", \n![Getty Images Volunteers clean Castle Beach in Tenby ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/a02a/live/9892d970-09d9-11f1-bfd9-a1813d3ca61e.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThousands of volunteers came to help clean up Pembrokeshire's beaches after the spill\n\"One of the long-term things that changed was people's attitude,\" said Nick Ainger, the area's MP at the time.\n\"Those images they saw - that's the real price of oil. And I think it was the beginning of this substantial change in public attitude towards the environment: how precious it was, and how easily it can be damaged if we're not sensible and caring about it.\"\n\"I think about it at every anniversary,\" added Iolo Williams. \"Thirty years on it's important to tell the story so we learn from our mistakes.\"\n_Additional reporting by Clare Hutchinson_\nMore top stories\n[Wales](https://www.bbc.com/news/wales)\n[Milford Haven](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c1kr68g2w1zt)\n[Pollution](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c40rjmqdq4xt)\n[Environment](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cnx753jenyjt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [My son's been repeatedly attacked by gangs since we moved to the UK](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cwygyl83el8o)\n## [Female athletes hit back at 'weird' and 'derogatory' comments about their appearance](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c24g1jyl3vmo)\n## [Wales' papers: 'Disgusting' scenes at Yr Wyddfa and builder's fake gas fitter](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cwy9p1v278lo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 2 hrs ago ![Dr Rob Morley led the research ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/a290/live/d8686050-07ff-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg) Antibiotic-resistant bacteria found in Oxford rivers Dr Rob Morley says a multi-agency effort is required to understand the implications of his research. ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cj0djn731eno)\n[ 2 hrs ago ![Volunteers at the site in Birling, Kent, planting hedgerows. video kidnapped Guthrie in the early hours of the morning of 1 February.\nThe FBI said on Sunday that investigators recovered the glove from a field near the side of the road about two miles from {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cn9exzl254xo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Why writer Arundhati Roy's cult classic film is still relevant in India\n![The Film Heritage Foundation A young Arundhati Roy wearing a yellow tunic and a black scarf bends slightly in front of a blackboard in a scene from In Which Annie Gives It Those Ones](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/10a7/live/9d718cc0-0826-11f1-aced-291774efe15a.jpg.webp)The Film Heritage Foundation\nStarring Roy herself, the film tells the story of a group of students navigating life in Delhi\nOn a sticky Delhi afternoon in the late 1980s, a group of architecture students loiter, argue and dream their way through a city that seems permanently unfinished. \nThey are idealistic and impatient, fluent in slogans and sarcasm, and astutely aware that the system they are training to enter may have no particular use for them.\nThis is the world of In Which Annie Gives It Those Ones, the 1989 television film written by Booker Prize-winning author Arundhati Roy and directed by Pradip Krishen. \nNearly four decades after it first aired on India's state broadcaster Doordarshan, a restored version of the film is set to receive its world premiere in the Berlinale Classics section of the 2026 Berlin International Film Festival.\nThe Film Heritage Foundation, which initiated its restoration, will also release the film in select theatres across India in March, pricing tickets low to attract younger viewers.\n\"We wanted it to be accessible,\" said Shivendra Singh Dungarpur, filmmaker and Director at Film Heritage Foundation. \"It's a significant film. In its dialogue, in its portrayal of college life, in the kinds of characters it foregrounded - it achieved something unusual.\"\nOften described as India's first English-language feature, Annie occupies a singular place in the country's cinema history: local in texture yet cosmopolitan in voice; modest in scale yet exacting in its writing.\nMade on a modest budget, the film follows a ragtag group of final-year students at Delhi's School of Planning and Architecture as they drift, stall and inch toward graduation. \nIts title comes from a Delhi University slang: to \"give it those ones\" is to perform one's usual act - bungles included.\nAt its centre is Anand \"Annie\" Grover, an endearing fifth-year student suspended between idealism and chronic distraction. \nHe keeps hens in his hostel room and dreams up improbable schemes to transform India - including planting trees along railway tracks, fertilised by waste from passing trains. Four years earlier, he scrawled a crude joke about the dean in the men's toilet; he has failed every exam since.\n![The Film Heritage Foundation Four students - a woman in a green tunic along with three men, dressed in black, yellow and a striped shirt, respectively - stand in a classroom in a scene of the film. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/fe79/live/c0d3c700-0826-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)The Film Heritage Foundation\nThe college satire was screened on Doordarshan, before it vanished into obscurity\nAround him orbits a constellation of classmates - caustic, thoughtful, restless - who debate Swiss-French architect Le Corbusier, Karl Marx and the price of cigarettes with equal seriousness. \nRoy herself appears as Radha, sharp and self-possessed. The ensemble cast also includes a young Shah Rukh Khan - it was the Bollywood star's screen debut.\nWhat stands out in Annie is its relaxed, unvarnished portrait of student life: messy hostel rooms, friends lounging on charpoys smoking and arguing about everything from bureaucracy to exams, and a breezy irreverence toward authority. \nStudents treat institutional rituals as a farce and openly mock their principal - calling him Yamdoot after the Hindu god of death - a blustering figure who addresses them as \"My dear donkey\".\nRadha, Roy's character, embodies the film's carefree defiance. She smokes beedis (hand-rolled cheap Indian cigarettes) and dresses boldly, pairing a sari with a rakish hat, capturing the free-spirited energy of the campus.\nIn her memoir Mother Mary Comes to Me, Roy explains that the script grew out of \"the wacky anarchy of that campus, the stoned, bombed-out students and the dialect of English that we spoke - an inventive mix of Hindi and English\".\nThe response to the film was electric. \nAt its first screening in Delhi, Roy recalls that \"students jammed into the hall and crowded on to the floor. Within a few minutes the audience began to yell, roar with laughter and wolf-whistle through the film. They recognised themselves, their language, their clothes, their jokes, their silliness.\"\n![The Film Heritage Foundation A young Shah Rukh Khan \\(on the right\\), seen in a white robe and a plaster and a sling in his right hand which seems to be broken. He is standing next to a shorter man, wearing a red t-shirt.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/e753/live/4080cbe0-0829-11f1-9742-a1ebf100a84a.jpg.webp)The Film Heritage Foundation\nThe film also has a small role by Bollywood superstar Shah Rukh Khan (R), who plays one of the students\nBeyond its campus setting, Annie captures a moment when Indian cinema - and state broadcasters - were relatively permissive about youth culture, language and social critique.\nIn the late 1980s, television allowed more room for satire and student life than it does today. In the decades since, films challenging social norms or scrutinising institutions have more often faced resistance from the censors. \nWhen Annie was first released, it won two National Awards - for best screenplay and best English-language feature - but largely vanished after its television premiere, a quiet late-night screening on Doordarshan. \nIts reputation, however, quietly grew.\nBootleg recordings circulated among architecture students and cinephiles, who came to regard it as one of the few credible English-language Indian films of its time.\n\"Nobody had really made a film about English-speaking students in India,\" Dungarpur said. \"Audiences weren't used to hearing English spoken so casually. But that's how students in [some] colleges speak to each other. Annie captured that without self-consciousness.\"\nHe adds that audiences also recognised the mood of a country on the cusp of [economic liberalisation](https://www.bbc.com/news/av/world-asia-india-57939341), when a government job could represent both security and suffocation. The debates staged in the film - about hierarchy, bureaucracy and institutional power - remain familiar.\n\"The issues the film speaks about are still prevalent,\" Dungarpur said. \"That's why it resonates. It hasn't dated in the way you might expect.\"\n![Film Heritage Foundation A still of Arundhati Roy, wearing silver danglers and a necklace and a black tank top, as she looks sideways ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/bafe/live/aa32d680-088f-11f1-afab-cd05cbee4d40.jpg.webp)Film Heritage Foundation\nRoy has said the film's script was based on her own experiences as a student in Delhi\nThe restoration of the film began almost by accident. While moving house, Krishen discovered trunks of scripts and papers and was about to junk them before a friend alerted Dungarpur, who retrieved the material and later proposed restoring Annie.\nThe process required painstaking, almost forensic, effort. Colours had faded; the soundtrack posed its own challenges.\n\"There's a scene towards the end where Roy is wearing a red sari,\" Dungarpur said. \"In the surviving print, the red had almost disappeared. We had to ask: what kind of red was it?\"\nSeen now, Annie feels almost like a prelude to Roy's later life as a feted novelist and one of India's [most polarising writers](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c3ggyz13m2po). And yet, the film is lighter on its feet than her later reputation might suggest. It jokes. It rambles. It allows its characters to be foolish and tender. \nAs Roy has \n\"Students were rebelling, but with hope and celebration, not anger. The film was about people finding joy in being themselves and saying we don't want to conform,\" says Krishen in the same interview.\nAs it returns to the screen, younger viewers may glimpse another era - flared trousers, drafting tables, cigarette smoke curling under fluorescent lights.\nBut they may also recognise something more enduring: the persistence of the very systems the film gently mocks.\n\"They'll see how cool that time was,\" Dungarpur said. \"But they'll also see how much of it feels familiar.\"\n[Asia](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c5rznn0nvvyt)\n[Film](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cg41ylwvgjyt)\n[Delhi](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cmmqq736zwyt)\n[India](https://www.bbc.com/news/world/asia/india)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Sydney police plead for return of man, 85, kidnapped by mistake ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c0ljzye1zgno)\n## ['Dragged out and set on fire' - {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cvgjw0kpd89o", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Are wetter winters and frequent flooding here to stay?\n![Ben Birchall/PA Wire An aerial photo showing a sea of flooded fields in Somerset and in the foreground is a hill with a church on the top](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/68fa/live/cceab760-0718-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Ben Birchall/PA Wire\nFlooded fields near Burrowbridge in Somerset this month\nAreas across the UK from Cornwall to County Down have seen their wettest January on record continue with heavy rain in February.\nThe deluge the country has been experiencing in the last week has been put down to a blocked weather pattern - a high pressure system over Scandinavia is preventing the wet weather from moving away.\nThe Met Office estimates that at current levels of global warming, wet winters like 2023/24 have gone from being once in 80-year events to once in 20 - and with further warming this could become even more frequent.\nThis could have significant impacts for housing, transport and food supply. \nOne farmer in Somerset told the BBC that he was living on a \"knife edge\" as his crops were days away from rotting in the floodwater.\n## Wetter winters more common\nOn Tuesday, more than 100 locations across the UK faced flood warnings and more than 300 homes had already succumbed to the flood waters, according to the Environment Agency (EA).\nThe heavy and continuous days of rainfall follow a similar pattern to the last few years of wetter winters. Six of the ten wettest since records began nearly 250 years ago have been this century, according to the Met Office.\n![A visual graphic of the UK showing rainfall across the UK in January 2026 as a percentage of the 1991 - 2020 average. Wetter areas are shown as dark blue and cover most of the country apart from East Scotland and North East of England](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d22e/live/72dbcc10-069f-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.png.webp)\nThe UK's rainfall is strongly influenced by natural variability, but the trend towards wetter winters is in line with predictions from the UK's meteorological organisation.\nIncreased burning of fossil fuels like coal and oil over the last two centuries has released greenhouse gases like CO2 into the atmosphere, which have warmed our planet.\nIt might not sound a lot, but for every 1C temperature rise our atmosphere can hold 7% more moisture \u2013 this can create heavier rainfall.\nAt the same time sea level rise around the UK is also accelerating, due to warmer, expanding oceans and melting glaciers.\nSince 1901, sea levels have increased by 20cm, which may not sound like a lot but coupled with extreme storm events and tidal surges can exacerbate coastal flooding.\n![Line graph showing the variation in total rainfall between December and February between 1836/37 and 2024/25. The lines are in blue and the trend line is black dashed, it shows a steady increase over the period, with slight fluctuations. The headline reads \"UK winters have been getting wetter\". The source is Met Office.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/eba1/live/8aa13fc0-0680-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.png.webp)\n## How much wetter could our winters get?\nCurrently the UK experiences about seven days a year where rainfall is more than 80mm a day \u2013 considered a heavy rainfall event. If 30mm of this falls in an hour it typically triggers a flash flooding warning.\nBut, according to the Met Office, if global temperatures rise by more than 2C (above pre-industrial levels) this would increase to nine days.\nEven with current policies to reduce our emissions, global temperatures are expected to have increased by at least 2.5C by the end of the century, according to the United Nations.\n![Graphic showing two maps of the UK side by side and how rainfall will change across the country in a 1.5C and 2C warming scenario. Title reads \"Predictions for UK winter rainfall in a warmer climate\"](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/47ed/live/44665350-0690-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.png.webp)\nAs well as more intense rainfall we are also seeing more rainfall clustered together, explained Prof Lizzie Kendon, head of climate projections at the Met Office. \n\"That is really important, because that can lead to successive rainfall events, [which] can lead to very saturated soils, and as we're seeing currently in the UK, that can lead to exacerbated flooding as well,\" she said. \n## What is the damage from wetter winters?\nHeavy deluges and saturated soils have the potential to cause significant damage to homes, transport infrastructure and food supply.\nIn December, the Environment Agency estimated that by 2050 one in four properties would be at risk from flooding. This is the first time the EA has considered how a warmer climate could affect flooding in the UK.\nThe East Midlands, Yorkshire and The Humber, and south-east England are particularly at risk.\nThis number, the EA said, would rise if more homes were built on floodplains. The UK government plans to build 1.5 million homes in this Parliament, and in some parts of the country \nThe same picture is seen across the transport network - currently a third of railways are at risk of flooding, which leads to cancellations and damaged infrastructure.\nIn less than 25 years this will rise to more than half of the rail network being at risk, \n![A man wearing a green jacket and checked shirt stands to the right of a grassy field which is submerged below flood water, the edge of a fence can be seen behind him. ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/2530/live/03d5bb90-0682-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)\nJames Winslade said nearly all of his farm has been flooded in the recent rains\nThe National Farmers Union (NFU) wrote to MPs in November last year to lay out the severe risks the farming sector was facing.\n\"Farmers and growers experience both [drought and floods] on an annual basis, and both severely impact their ability to produce food,\" it said.\nThe wet winter of 2024 \u2013 the second wettest on record \u2013 saw the sector experience \u00a31bn losses from damaged crops.\nJames Winslade, a beef and arable farmer from Somerset, has seen more than 90% of his farm submerged in the recent rains, and said he is days away from losing his crops to rot.\n\"We can't protect everywhere. There isn't insurance for crop damage. We don't get compensation.\"\nHe said he is a third-generation farmer, and that his father and grandfather never saw this level and recurrence of flooding.\nThe UK does have an extensive network of flood defences but there is a patchwork of organisations - including farmers, water companies and charities - responsible for them.\nHalf of flood defences \u2013 around 100,000 - are maintained by the Environment Agency and 9% of those are currently below their target condition. Each defence is given a score out of five they must achieve depending on their importance. \nBut previous analysis by the BBC Shared Data Unit estimated that the defences not maintained by the EA were 45% more likely to be below target.\nAnd increased development will only exacerbate flooding \u2013 concreting over surfaces for housing or car parks prevents rain being absorbed and instead it runs off into drains and rivers which can become overwhelmed and breach defences.\nAn Environment Agency spokesperson told the BBC: \"As a result of climate change, we are seeing more flooding and extreme weather. Whilst it is sadly not possible to stop all flooding, the Environment Agency is committed to helping communities to adapt.\n\"Through the government's flood programme a further \u00a310.5bn [will be] invested in protecting 900,000 more properties by 2036.\"\n_With additional reporting from Mark Poynting, Jonah Fisher, Miho Tanaka and Tom Ingham._\n![Thin, green banner promoting the Future Earth newsletter with text saying, \u201cThe world\u2019s biggest climate news in your inbox every week\u201d. There is also a graphic of an iceberg overlaid with a green circular pattern.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8c6c/live/f9295fc0-0fde-11f0-ba12-8d27eb561761.png.webp)\n[Sign up to our international newsletter here](https://cloud.email.bbc.com/FutureEarth_Newsletter_Signup).\n[Floods](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c26xdmnee19t)\n[Climate](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cmj34zmwm1zt)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Ice and flood warnings for parts of northern UK as cold weather continues](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cdxgew324r9o)\n## [London crews to the rescue in Mozambique floods](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cz9qgelpn1go)\n## [Flooding caused by poor maintenance, says alliance](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c043l3dd4nwo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 2 days ago ![A view of wind turbines against a clear blue sky.
