{"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cx2k23mlkzlo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Ex-church minister who admitted child sexual abuse to BBC still free years later\n![BBC Promo image shows Michael Havet in the 1980s with a stock image of someone praying in the background.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/c246/live/6d904160-0419-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.png.webp)BBC\nAn ex-minister of a shadowy Christian church who told the BBC he sexually abused a child in Canada is still free more than two years after he made his admission.\nRobert Corfield admitted when confronted by the BBC that he sexually abused a boy, Michael Havet, in the 1980s. Corfield's was one of more than 1,100 names given to a hotline set up to report sexual abuse within the church, which has no official name but is often referred to as The Truth or the Two by Twos.\nThe FBI launched a probe into the church around a month after the BBC published its investigation in early 2024, but Corfield remains free in the US state of Montana despite him saying investigators visited him more than a year ago.\nWe have now spoken to a man who says he was also sexually abused by Corfield in 1974 when he was 11 years old - around a decade before he started abusing Michael. Corfield previously claimed to the BBC that he had not sexually abused anyone else.\nThe FBI did not respond to requests for comment.\nAsked about Michael's case, the Royal Canadian Mounted Police (RCMP) said it launched an investigation after it \"received a report of historic sexual assaults that occurred in the early 1980s\" in Saskatchewan and that its \"findings were sent to Crown Prosecutors for their assessment\".\nThe Saskatchewan Ministry of Justice said it \"does not comment on whether it is reviewing cases under investigation by the police\".\nThe Truth is believed to have up to 100,000 members worldwide, with the majority in North America.\nIt was founded in Ireland by a Scottish evangelist in 1897 and is built around ministers - referred to by the church as workers - spreading New Testament teachings through word-of-mouth.\nOne of its hallmarks is that workers give up their possessions and must be taken in by church members as they travel around, spreading the gospel. This makes children living in the homes they visit vulnerable to abuse, former members say.\n_Warning: This article contains details some readers may find upsetting_\nContacted by phone in December 2025, Corfield told the BBC that he was visited by the FBI a year ago.\n\"They saw me in person. They basically reviewed information and I just responded with the truth,\" he said.\nAsked whether he told them that he abused Michael, now 57, for six years starting when he was 12, Corfield replied \"yes\" before adding: \"It was a year ago - I don't remember all the details.\"\nHe added that the FBI \"were satisfied at that point and I haven't been contacted by anyone else since\".\nAsked if he was fearful of arrest, Corfield said: \"I realise there is that possibility.\"\nHe was no longer attending church meetings, he added.\n![Photo supplied Robert Corfield, left, and Michael Havet, right, are seen in the 1980s. A person in the middle is blurred out.\n](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/e74b/live/8a9c03f0-05a1-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Photo supplied\nRobert Corfield, left, told the BBC he sexually abused Michael Havet, right, for six years.\nLast month, another man contacted the BBC about being abused by Corfield.\nThe man, who is entitled to anonymity but we will call Edward, said that Corfield attacked him on a camping trip in Canada in 1974.\n\"Robert got mad at me for something - I don't remember exactly what that was. Then he called me into the camper,\" he said.\n\"I can only remember him being angry and holding me down. I just remember the cushions being covered with that weird 70s floral pattern \u2026 he ended up holding me down on the bed,\" he added.\n\"He went from angry to sexually assaulting me.\"\nEdward recalled another time when a group of boys were swimming on church convention grounds and he saw Corfield watching, \"blanket over his lap, doing something inappropriate to himself underneath the blanket\".\nCorfield \"was in constant grooming mode\", Edward said.\nHe tried to tell his parents about what Corfield did to him, but said \"they wouldn't even hear a name from me\".\nThe power of the workers in the church means they are rarely challenged.\n\"When my incident happened with Robert, I tried to talk to my parents about it and neither one of them would have anything to do with that, because he was a preacher and he could do no wrong,\" Edward said.\n\"Our house was an open door, the workers had keys to the house,\" he added.\n\"They were on the pedestal, they were the moral compass. The family kind of didn't have a moral compass on its own, they only only looked at right and wrong based on what the workers said. Right and wrong wasn't about what your heart or soul said.\"\nEdward reported the attack to the RCMP in 2024 but the prosecutor's office declined to prosecute the case due to lack of evidence.\nCorfield declined to comment about the latest allegations when contacted last week.\nHowever, the BBC has obtained two private letters Corfield sent to Michael, in 2004 and 2005, which asked for forgiveness and said he was seeing a therapist. In one letter, Corfield said he was \"making a list of victims\".\n![Photo supplied Michael Havet is seen today, stroking a black dog.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/0907/live/3d424c30-0417-11f1-97a0-c37ebf10ce5f.jpg.webp)Photo supplied\nMichael, now 57, says he also wants to see the church's leadership to face justice\nThe church has been thrown into crisis since more than 1,500 current and former members of the sect contacted a hotline set up by campaign group, Advocates for The Truth, in 2023.\nThe Advocates have since ceased operations, but co-founder and private investigator, Cynthia Liles, is still running the hotline under the name 2x2 Church Accountability.\nThere have been 1,164 allegations of sexual abuse, she said, with more than half of the accused in positions of authority. There have been approximately 75 convictions, she said.\nBut despite Corfield admitting to sexually abusing a child for six years, he remains free.\n\"I'm confused by the law enforcement in Canada. Robert is a confessed pedophile\u2026he took responsibility for abusing me for six years,\" Michael told the BBC in a message.\nHe also wants to see the church's leadership face justice, some of whom have been accused of covering up abuse.\nIn our previous investigation, Michael said he reported his abuse in 1993 to Dale Shultz, Saskatchewan's most senior church leader - known as an \"overseer\". Overseers are the most senior members of the church and there is one for each US state and Canadian province where there is an active following.\nBut Shultz didn't go to the police and Michael says he violently assaulted him a few weeks later because he thought he had told others of the abuse claims.\nMichael said Shultz then \"encouraged\" him to leave the church - while Corfield was moved to be a minister across the border, in the US state of Montana.\nCorfield told the BBC in 2024 that he believed it was Shultz's decision to send him to Montana, where he remained in post for 25 years.\nShultz previously told the BBC that \"much of the information that you have received concerning me is distorted and inaccurate\". However he declined to go into any further detail.\n\"I'm frustrated that the RCMP refused to include the leadership. They failed the membership,\" Michael said last week.\n\"Robert is guilty, sure. Dale and all the enablers are guilty also,\" he added.\nEdward said \"it's completely ridiculous\" that Corfield is still free.\n\"What kind of pressure does the RCMP need to feel to make this move forward?\" he said.\nThe RCMP directed questions to the Saskatchewan Ministry of Justice, who declined to comment.\nThe church itself has done little to address the sexual abuse crisis, said Liles, the private investigator, who herself was born into the sect before leaving.\n\"In fact I think they're going backwards,\" she said. \"I think they retracted from positions they originally took in an attempt to show that they were going to try to make changes.\"\nSome states initially hired an organisation that helps Christian groups confront sexual abuses, she said, but the church ended up rejecting the policies.\n\"They just said: 'we don't need any of this, we're just going to use the Bible,'\" Liles said.\nMany perpetrators of sexual abuse are back in church meetings, Liles said, including one man in Arizona who was jailed for rape in 1969, made a church elder upon release and then went on to abuse other children within the church.\nThe BBC reached out to multiple church overseers for comment, but did not get a response.\nWhile the wheels of justice are moving slowly for victims, many are connecting online. One Facebook group called 'Exposing Abuse: 2x2s' has more than 10,000 members from across the world offering each other support.\nAfter Corfield's public confession two years ago, Edward reached out to Michael.\n\"I reached out to Michael very, very soon after that and we managed to stay in contact over the last few years and develop a relationship,\" he said.\n\"For us to know each other, it gets rid of that isolated 'me against the world' feeling - it's actually us against the church.\"\nEdward is also now receiving counselling to work through his experiences in the church.\n\"It's not the church that's helping to fund my counselling sessions, it's a bunch of ex-church members,\" he said.\n\"That's been a godsend for me.\"\n[Sexual violence](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c1038wnxe18t)\n[Child abuse](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c46zkd8y97et)\n[Canada](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cywd23g0q41t)\n* * *\nRelated\n## [Queen's letter of support left Pelicot 'overwhelmed' ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/ckgl9vljk13o)\n## [Watch: 'I am a survivor' Gis\u00e8le Pelicot tells BBC Newsnight ](https://www.bbc.com/news/videos/c80jvkem8lro)\n## [Man admits rape of former councillor's ex-wife](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c3rzqw7vxpzo)\n* * *\nMore from the BBC\n[ 20 hrs ago ![Two men walk past departures board at Beijing airport](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/f3cb/live/db4e5850-0a67-11f1-ac70-650e103cdf93.jpg) China confirms visa-free travel for UK and Canadian nationals Relaxed visa rules for British and Canadian passport holders will come into force on 17 February.  ](https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c875d3d3x34o)\n[ 2 days ago ![Elliot Jones is looking at the camera in a police custody photo. {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/crrnyg9e91jo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# The science of soulmates: Is there someone out there exactly right for you?\n![BBC A treated image of a glass beaker with two hearts inside](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/dbbb/live/a942e2b0-01b4-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)BBC\n**On Valentine's Day, there's the temptation to believe that somewhere out there is \"The One\": a soulmate, a perfect match, the person you were meant to be with.**\nAcross history, humans have always been drawn to the idea that love isn't random. In ancient Greece, Plato imagined that we were once whole beings with four arms, four legs and two faces, so radiant that Zeus split us in two; ever since, each half has roamed the earth searching for its missing other, a myth that gives the modern soulmate its poetic pedigree and the promise that somewhere, someone will finally make us feel complete.\nIn the Middle Ages, troubadours and Arthurian tales recast that longing as \"courtly love\", a fierce, often forbidden devotion like Lancelot's for Guinevere, in which a knight proved his worth through self-sacrifice for a beloved he might never openly declare.\n![Getty Images Two treated images of a drawing of Plato and a close up image of a statue of Zeus.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8434/live/5e35de30-05c9-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nPlato (left) imagined humans were once whole, with four arms, four legs and two faces, before Zeus (right) split them, leaving each half searching for its other\nBy the Renaissance, writers such as Shakespeare were talking of \"star-crossed lovers\", couples bound together by an overwhelming connection yet pulled apart by family, fortune or fate, as if the universe itself both wrote their love story and barred them from a happy ending.\nIn more recent times, Hollywood and romance novels have sold us fairy tale love stories.\nBut what does the latest science say about soulmates? Is there a particular special someone out there for us?\n## How we fall for 'The One'\nViren Swami, Professor of Social Psychology at Anglia Ruskin University (ARU), in Cambridge, has traced our contemporary European understanding of romantic love back to medieval Europe and those stories of Camelot, Lancelot, Guinevere and the chivalry of the knights of the round table that swept across the continent.\n\"These stories first pushed the idea that you should choose one other individual as your companion and that companion is for life,\" he says.\n\"Before that, in much of Europe, you could love as many people as you like, and love was fluid, and it was often not about sex.\"\nOver time, as people are uprooted from their agricultural communities as industrialisation tears apart people's familiar attachments, individuals become \"alienated\", he says. \"They start looking for one other individual to save them, to save them from the wretchedness of their lives.\"\n![Getty Images A drawing of Lancelot and Guinevere](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/667e/live/6922a6c0-05c9-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nViren Swami believes today's ideas about romantic love can be traced back to medieval European stories like Lancelot and Guinevere\nToday's dating apps turn that story into an algorithm, which Swami calls \"relation-shopping\". The search for a soulmate turns into the opposite of what they are looking for: \"For many people, that's a really soulless experience.\n\"You're shopping for a partner\u2026 going through possibly dozens of people on the dating app until you get to a point where you go\u2026 I need to stop,\" he says.\n## The One\nJason Carroll, Professor of Marriage and Family Studies at the US Brigham Young University, based in Provo, Utah, is sympathetic to the longing for \"The One\".\n\"We are attachment-based creatures,\" he says. \"We desire that bond.\" But in his lectures, he tells students they need to leave the idea of a soulmate, without giving up their desire for The One.\nIt sounds like a contradiction, but for Carroll, it's the difference between destiny and graft.\n\"A soulmate is just simply found. It's already pre\u2011made. But a one and only is something two people carve out together over years of adapting, apologising, and occasionally gritting their teeth,\" he says.\n## Soulmate trap\nCarroll's argument draws on decades of research, which he put together in his report, The Soulmate Trap, much of which distinguishes between what psychologists call \"destiny beliefs\" - the idea that the right relationship should feel effortless - and \"growth beliefs\", which focus on what partners can do to make things work.\nIn a widely cited series of studies in the late 1990s and early 2000s led by Professor C. Raymond Knee at the University of Houston, researchers found that people who believed relationships were \"meant to be\" were far more likely to doubt their commitment after conflict. Those with more growth-minded views tended to stay more committed, even on days when they argued.\nThose with growth-based views, Carroll argues, still want something special, but expect rough patches. \"They ask\u2026 what can they do to make their relationship better, have improvement and have growth?\"\n![Getty Images A silhouette of a romantic couple](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d880/live/6f422fd0-05c9-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nResearch suggests people with \"growth\" beliefs about relationships still want something special, but expect challenges along the way\nIn his view, the soulmate belief is a trap - not the romance itself, but the expectation that love should never be hard. The most \"soulful\" part of a long relationship, he says, is not a cinematic charge, but having \"front-row seats not only for each other's strengths, but... [their] challenges and weaknesses\".\n\"That's a pretty sacred space,\" he says. \"We only know those things because they've let us be there.\"\nFor Carroll, when love is treated as fate, people become less willing to do the unshowy work that actually keeps love alive. Carroll says the soulmate trap makes it much harder when a relationship hits its first serious snag.\n\"The first time there's any type of struggle, the immediate thought is, 'well, I thought you were my soulmate. But maybe you're not, because soulmates aren't supposed to deal with things',\" he says. \"But if relationships are going to go long term, it's never just going to be a downhill run.\"\n## Spark or trauma?\nVicki Pavitt, a London-based love coach, often helps people who thought they'd found their soulmate, only to discover that the fairy tale came with emotional manipulation, flakiness, and a constant sense of anxiety.\n\"When there is a lot of chemistry and the spark, I think that can sometimes be about opening old unhealthy patterns, like old wounds\", she says.\n\"A person who is inconsistent or plays a bit hot and cold can make you feel 'I can't wait to see them again', but what's really happening is they're giving you so much anxiety and that it has you wanting more\".\n![Vicki Pavitt A shot of Vicki Pavitt wearing a white suit with a black top.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/aa5c/live/75cd5fd0-0675-11f1-b7e1-afb6d0884c18.jpg.webp)Vicki Pavitt\nLove coach Vicki Pavitt often works with people who thought they'd found a soulmate, only to find the relationship brought anxiety\nPavitt says what we feel to be destiny may be a pull from our nervous system recognising something that hurt us before and trying to fix it, a pattern therapists call a trauma bond.\nThis bond can seem like love, she says, and leads to people magnetically drawn into unhealthy dynamics because they are familiar, not because they are the perfect match.\nOne study often cited is by Canadian psychologists Donald Dutton and Susan Painter. In research published in 1993 while they were at the University of British Columbia, they followed 75 women after they had left abusive partners.\nThe team measured how strongly the women still felt attached to their exes and compared this with what their relationships had been like.\nThey found the strongest bonds were not in women who had consistently been abused, but in those whose partners alternated between charm and cruelty.\n![Getty Images Two people tie a red ribbon to their little fingers](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/8945/live/b4da1f10-0675-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nPavitt says what feels like destiny can sometimes be a trauma bond\nDutton and Painter argue that this trauma bond helps explain why people can feel magnetised back to relationships that are objectively bad for them \u2013 because the mix of danger and affection is familiar, not because it is healthy.\nIt is that distinction Pavitt tries to surface in coaching: \"It's about discerning whether the chemistry you feel is showing me this person's compatible with me or if it is a familiar sense of anxiety.\n\"In my language, I never talk about soulmates,\" she says. \"I don't personally believe that there is one person for everybody... but I do believe that we become \"The One\" for someone.\"\n## Real chemistry\nIf ruling out the existence of a soulmate sounds unromantic, the biology of attraction points in the same direction.\nHormonal contraceptives may subtly reshape how partners feel about each other. Research suggests that pills which flatten the natural ebb and flow of fertility can dampen shifts in attraction that typically occur across the menstrual cycle, potentially altering initial mate choice.\n![PA Wire A pharmacist stocks shelves at a chemist.](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/63f1/live/3ba572e0-05c9-11f1-9972-d3f265c101c6.jpg.webp)PA Wire\nSome research suggests hormonal contraception may subtly influence attraction in partners\nOne large study of 365 hetrosexual couples found that women's sexual satisfaction was higher when their current contraceptive status matched what it was when they first chose their partner, hinting that changes in pill use can change how a partner is experienced. These effects are small but could help explain some couples' puzzling shifts in chemistry over time.\nIf hormones and pills can tilt who feels like \"The One\", then it becomes harder to argue there is a single, pre\u2011ordained match \u2013 which is where the mathematicians come in.\n## The one but not the only\nPsychology and biology offer one way of thinking about \"The One\", but mathematics puts forward another.\nDr Greg Leo, an economist at Vanderbilt University in Nashville, Tennessee, has come up with a compatibility algorithm. It finds that not only might you have a \"One\" you have lots of \"Ones\".\nIn his Matching Soulmates paper in the journal of Public Economic Theory, everyone is in a computer simulated dating pool, where thousands of digitally created daters rank each other. His algorithm picks \"first\u2011order soulmates\": pairs who choose each other in a stable matching. It removes them, and runs it again with those left, and you get second\u2011order soulmates, and so on.\nMore from InDepth\nIn his simulations, it was extremely rare for someone to have their mutual first picks; but many people had those that were second or third picks. In this scenario a couple counts as happy if each is near the top of the other's list and neither can find someone they and that other person would both prefer more.\nIt may only be number crunching, but the love algorithm tells us that there are many viable partners, not just The One.\n## Sweat the small stuff\nSo how can a couple co-create their Ones?\nJacqui Gabb, Professor of Sociology and Intimacy at The Open University, assessed this in her Enduring Love project, published in the journal Sociology in 2015.\nIt surveyed around 5,000 people and then followed 50 couples in forensic, sometimes intrusive detail, combining statistics with diaries, interviews and \"emotion maps\" of what happened in the home.\nWhen she asked people what made them feel appreciated, it wasn't sunset proposals or surprise trips to Paris.\nIt was \"surprise gifts, thoughtful gestures and the kindness of a cup of tea in bed\". Warming the car on a cold morning. Picking wildflowers and putting them in a vase. Sharing a private smile at a party.\nQuantitatively, what she describes as these \"everyday attentive acts\" turned out to be much more powerful than grand romantic gestures.\nIn her survey, 22% of mothers and 20% of childless women picked such small gestures as one of the top two things that made them feel valued \u2013 more than big nights out or expensive presents.\nRelationship satisfaction in the data wasn't primarily about money or romance; it was about \"intimate couple knowledge\" and its expression in daily life.\nIn one young couple's diary given to them for the project, Sumaira describes her partner coming home, the dinner she has cooked, the hug in the hallway, the two of them eating together at the table.\n\"It's perfect,\" she writes in her research diary. \"Just us and food. What more could I want?\"\nThen there is a spontaneous dance in the living room, a walk in long grass where she gets scared of the dark, and a photo her partner loves so much he makes it the background on his phone.\nIt reads like a lovely every day tale, not a fairy tale: no glass slippers, but wellies.\nYet Gabb points out that woven through the sweetness are money worries, family obligations and a history of depression that the couple are learning to manage together.\n\"The soulmate feeling here doesn't float above life; it is made, inch by inch, by life, in the way the pair meet those pressures,\" she says.\n## Valentine's Dinner\nThe science doesn't steal the romance \u2013 rather it's there to help it bloom, in good times and bad, according to Carroll.\n\"I'm pretty comfortable with the aspiration to be in a unique special relationship as long as we remember it needs to be created,\" he says.\nPavitt thinks \"it's fine, helpful, even to have faith that your person is out there, so long as you know there are many people that you could form a really great connection with and stop expecting anyone to be perfect\".\nAs for soulmates, the science points to a paradox. The people who end up with relationships that feel uniquely \"meant to be\" are often the ones who stopped waiting for fate, turned towards the flawed person in front of them and said, in effect: shall we make something of this?\n_Additional reporting by Florence Freeman_\n_Top image credit: Getty Images_\n![Thin, lobster red banner with white text saying \u2018InDepth newsletter\u2019. Epstein files have been released\n![Getty Images Pam Bondi ](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/d134/live/2a2760c0-0ab8-11f1-99f8-175d6377faa5.jpg.webp)Getty Images\nThe US Department of Justice (DoJ) has said it has released all of the files required by the Epstein Files Transparency Act, but lawmakers have argued the release is insufficient.\nThe letter sent to members of the US Congress on Saturday from US Attorney General Pam Bondi and her deputy said all documents held by the DoJ had been released and contained a list of names appearing in the files.\nKentucky Republican Representative Thomas Massie, who co-wrote the law, called for the DoJ to also release internal memos outlining past decisions on whether to charge Jeffrey Epstein and his associates.\nMillions of new files relating to the late sex offender Epstein were released by the DoJ earlier this month.\nIn the letter, Bondi and her deputy Todd Blanche wrote: \"In accordance with the requirements of the Act, 'records, documents, communications and investigative materials in the possession of the Department' that 'relate to' any of nine different categories.\"\nNo records were withheld from the DoJ's release \"on the basis of embarrassment, reputational harm, or political sensitivity\", the letter added. \nThe individuals listed in the letter include people who \"are or were a government official or politically exposed person\", and whose name appeared at least once in the files, the letter says. \nThe names appear in the files in a \"wide variety of contexts\", including some people who had \"extensive direct email contact with Epstein or Maxwell\" and others who were merely referenced in documents or news articles contained in the files, Blanche and Bondi wrote. \n![Getty Images There is no suggestion that appearing in the documents implies any wrongdoing and each has denied any involvement in Epstein's crimes. \nBut the list also includes the names of deceased musicians Janis Joplin and Elvis Presley. \nThe letter was addressed to the Senate Judiciary Committee's chairman Chuck Grassley and ranking member Dick Durbin, as well as the House Judiciary Committee's chairman Jim Jordan and ranking member Jamie Raskin.\nIn an interview on ABC This Week on Sunday, Massie argued that, even though the DoJ \"wants to say they are done with this document production\", there are more crucial files that should be released. \nThe DoJ was \"signing deliberative process privilege in order not to release some of the documents\", he said.\n\"The problem with that is the bill that Ro Khanna and I wrote says that they must release internal memos and notes and emails about their decisions on whether to prosecute or not prosecute, whether to investigate or not investigate.\"\nIn response to the letter, California Democratic Representative Ro Khanna, who co-wrote the Epstein Files Transparency Act, accused the DoJ of \"purposefully muddying the waters on who was a predator and who was mentioned in an email\".\n\"To have Janis Joplin, who died when Epstein was 17, in the same list as Larry Nassar, who went to prison for the sexual abuse of hundreds of young women and child pornography, with no clarification of how either was mentioned in the files is absurd,\" Khanna wrote on X on Saturday. \n\"Release the full files,\" he added. \"Stop protecting predators. Redact only the survivor's names.\"\nThe BBC has contacted the Department of Justice for comment. \nUS lawmakers previously said files on Epstein were improperly redacted ahead of their release, leading to at least one document being unredacted after their criticism. \nLawyers for Epstein's victims have also said the latest tranche of files included email addresses and nude photos in which the names and faces of potential victims could be identified. \nAt the time, the DoJ said mistakes were due to \"technical or human error\" and they had taken down all the flagged files. \n## [US {"url": "https://www.bbc.com/news/videos/crmlgpnnzyxo", "depth": 1, "published_time": null, "markdown": "[](https://www.bbc.com/)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/sport)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/health)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/weather)\n[](https://www.bbc.com/newsletters)\n# Tribes grant the Colorado River legal personhood - can this help save it?\nIndigenous tribes in the southwestern US, led by the Colorado River Indian Tribes (CRIT), have granted the Colorado River legal personhood to help protect it for future generations. In November 2025, the CRIT, made up of the Mojave, Hopi, Navajo and Chemehuevi tribes, passed the resolution recognising the river as a living, life-giving entity with rights like a person. \nThe Colorado River is currently facing the worst drought in 1,200 years. \nThe seven US states that share the river have until 14 February to reach a new water sharing agreement before current ones expires at the end of 2026, or the federal government will step in with their own plan. CRIT believe that personhood status will be a start to help protect the river.\n2 days ago\n[Colorado](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c008ql15d2et)\n[Nevada](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c50znx8v4jpt)\n[Wyoming](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c50znx8v4ypt)\n[Native Americans](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c8nq32jwnglt)\n[Drought](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/c8z0lk3537et)\n[Rivers](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cgg43v9jz7lt)\n[Climate](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cmj34zmwm1zt)\n[New Mexico](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cp7r8vgl2zlt)\n[United States](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cx1m7zg01xyt)\n[Arizona](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cx1m7zg0wm7t)\n[Utah](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cywd23g04yqt)\n[California](https://www.bbc.com/news/topics/cz4pr2gd5mzt)\n* * *\n[ ![Astronauts floating about and waving onboard in the International Space Station](https://ichef.bbci.co.uk/news/480/cpsprodpb/0cf5/live/28ba8630-09fb-11f1-b5e2-dd58fc65f0f6.jpg.webp) Watch: Moment crew docks at International Space Station The crew blasted off from Cape Canaveral in a SpaceX Dragon spacecraft atop a Falcon 9 rocket.
